38	\section*{Limitations}
39	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
40	\item \textbf{Model coverage.} Primary experiments use Qwen3-32B; cross-model validation (LLaMA-8B, LLaMA-70B, Gemini Flash) covers Dimensions~1--2 (count curves, BCS-controlled extremes) but does not replicate secondary analyses (error taxonomy, prompt sensitivity, TOST equivalence). Specifically, TOST factorial interaction (\S\ref{sec:multi_channel}) and SDGD failure analysis (\S\ref{sec:sdgd}) are established on Qwen3-32B alone; error taxonomy replicates on two additional models (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_error_taxonomy}), but TOST and SDGD cross-model replication remains the most pressing extension. Gemini provides only partial, leverage-sensitive corroboration (leave-one-out $r{:}\;{-}0.80 \to {-}0.42$; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_cross_model_gemini}); proprietary-model generalization remains open.
41	
42	\item \textbf{Effect size and statistical design.} Pairwise SCS explains a small fraction of type confusion variance; entity frequency, prompt position, and mention ambiguity likely account for the remainder. The count $\times$ BCS interaction is bounded on FewRel but nonzero on DocRED, partially confounded by BCS range restriction at higher counts (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}); logit-scale evidence remains inconclusive. DocRED count curves use 5 batches per level due to higher per-batch evaluation cost. BCS-controlled experiments use a single seed with greedy decoding; residual variance is small (Appendix~\ref{app:variance-decomposition}) but precise magnitudes may vary. Greedy decoding (temperature${}\,=\,0$) yields residual SD${}\,=\,0.88$\,pp, approximately 18--44\% of the observed BCS effect size, suggesting effect detection is robust but point estimates carry meaningful uncertainty; the factorial interaction point estimate (0.47\,pp) is far inside the $\pm 3$\,pp equivalence margin.
43	
44	\item \textbf{Task scope.} Format independence is confirmed via paired constrained/unconstrained comparison (Appendix~\ref{app:format-independence}). BCS batch-level prediction is task-dependent: significant in RE but not detected in NER at current power, where errors scatter across miss, boundary, and type modes rather than concentrating in type substitution. A post-hoc relaxed-matching evaluation confirms that relaxing the span criterion does not recover the BCS signal (Appendix~\ref{app:relaxed-matching}), ruling out evaluation stringency as the explanation. The NER batch-level BCS null is observed on three models (8B, 32B, 70B; Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_ner_null}) but not validated on Gemini; the null could in principle differ for proprietary models.
45	
46	\item \textbf{Causal mechanism and data contamination.} The study establishes correlational evidence with controlled stratification; mechanistic analysis at the attention or logit level is needed to confirm the causal pathway. A post-hoc renaming experiment (Appendix~\ref{app:renaming}) suggests SCS may proxy deeper conceptual overlap rather than surface similarity. FewRel (2018) may appear in pretraining corpora, but train-origin relations show lower accuracy than val-origin, opposite to the contamination hypothesis; DocRED (2019) provides a lower-contamination-risk replication (Appendix~\ref{app:split-origin}).
47	\item \textbf{Domain and language scope.} All evaluations use English-language datasets derived from Wikipedia or knowledge bases (Wikidata, FrameNet). Whether confusability effects hold for domain-specific schemas (e.g., biomedical NER, legal RE) or languages with different naming conventions is untested. BCS-controlled batches inherit the domain distribution of the source schema; domain-specific type subsets may shift the confusability--accuracy relationship. Sample sizes (1{,}000 instances per dataset) are sufficient for our primary effects but the pairwise SCS correlation approaches the detection floor.
48	\end{enumerate}
49	
50	\paragraph{Across-batch correlation.} Batches share prompt template and LLM, introducing potential across-batch correlation; however, type composition is independently sampled per batch, and bootstrap CIs are computed over this independent unit of variation.
51	
52	\paragraph{Multiple comparisons.} Few-NERD, FewRel, and DocRED draw from overlapping knowledge bases (Wikipedia, Wikidata), limiting dataset independence; we treat cross-dataset consistency as converging evidence rather than independent statistical replication \citep{rothman-1990} (noting that within-study prompt comparisons involve correlated observations rather than independent tests). Within-dataset secondary analyses involve multiple looks; we report individual $p$-values and note that core claims rest on $p < 0.001$ \citep{dror-2018-significance,card-2020-power}.
53

155	\section{SDGD Full Protocol and Results}
156	\label{sec:appendix_sdgd}
157	
158	We construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\textsc{largest\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent (i.e., highly confusable) types share a color. Each color class forms one extraction batch, and predictions are merged via set union.
159	
160	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
161	\item Compute pairwise SCS matrix $S_{ij} = \cos(\text{embed}(t_i), \text{embed}(t_j))$.
162	\item Construct graph $G$: add edge $(t_i, t_j)$ iff $S_{ij} > \tau$.
163	\item Apply greedy graph coloring (\textsc{largest\_first}) to assign each type a color $c(t)$.
164	\item For each color $k$, form batch $B_k = \{t : c(t) = k\}$ and run extraction on all test instances.
165	\item Merge: union of per-batch predictions (v1) or confidence-gated selection (v2).
166	\end{enumerate}
167	Oracle routing (Table~\ref{tab:routing_strategies}) recovers substantial accuracy (72.4\% vs.\ 50.4\% single-pass), confirming the sub-schemas contain correct predictions; the failure lies in routing, not decomposition quality.
168	
169	We compare four modes: (i)~\textbf{single\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\textbf{sdgd\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\textbf{random\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\textbf{per\_type}, the extreme case where each type is extracted in isolation.
170	
171	Single-pass extraction achieves F1\,=\,0.445 (P\,=\,0.563, R\,=\,0.368). SDGD obtains F1\,=\,0.251 (P\,=\,0.210, R\,=\,0.314), a 44\% relative drop. Random batching reaches F1\,=\,0.320 (P\,=\,0.249, R\,=\,0.446), and per-type extraction yields F1\,=\,0.292 (P\,=\,0.194, R\,=\,0.586). Every decomposition mode falls below the single-pass baseline (Table~\ref{tab:sdgd_results}).
172	
173	\begin{table}[h]
174	\centering
175	\small
176	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
177	\begin{tabular}{lrccc}
178	\toprule
179	\textbf{Mode} & \textbf{\# Batches} & \textbf{Precision} & \textbf{Recall} & \textbf{F1} \\
180	\midrule
181	single\_pass & 1 & 0.563 & 0.368 & 0.445 \\
182	\midrule
183	sdgd\_zero (v1) & 8 & 0.210 & 0.314 & 0.251 \\
184	random\_batch (v1) & 8 & 0.249 & 0.446 & 0.320 \\
185	per\_type & 66 & 0.194 & 0.586 & 0.292 \\
186	\midrule
187	sdgd\_zero (v2) & 4 & 0.324 & 0.191 & 0.241 \\
188	random\_batch (v2) & 8 & 0.331 & 0.276 & 0.301 \\
189	\bottomrule
190	\end{tabular}}
191	\caption{Few-NERD extraction under decomposition modes. V1 uses union merge; v2 uses confidence-gated merge with \texttt{min\_batch\_size=8} (reducing 8$\to$4 batches for sdgd\_zero). All decomposition modes produce lower F1 than the single-pass baseline. All modes use the v1 prompt.}
192	\label{tab:sdgd_results}
193	\end{table}
194	
195	Decomposition boosts recall (per-type recall reaches 0.586 versus single-pass 0.368) but devastates precision (0.194 versus 0.563). When the same text is presented to the model $k$ times with different type subsets, each pass independently generates entity predictions. Because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes, the union merge step accumulates overlapping and contradictory predictions and inflates false positives.
196	
197	Our v2 merge ranks competing predictions by the LLM's self-reported confidence and retains only the highest-scoring candidate for each span. Confidence gating improves precision (sdgd\_zero: 0.210$\to$0.324; random\_batch: 0.249$\to$0.331), but at the cost of disproportionate recall loss (0.314$\to$0.191; 0.446$\to$0.276), and both modes produce lower F1 (sdgd\_zero: 0.251$\to$0.241; random\_batch: 0.320$\to$0.301).
198

226	\section{Factorial Analysis and Separability Test}
227	\label{sec:appendix_factorial}
228	
229	To formally test whether count and confusability effects are additive, we fit a multiple regression (accuracy $\sim$ $\log_2$\,count $+$ BCS $+$ count$\times$BCS) across 69 batches from FewRel spanning five count levels ($k \in \{4, 8, 16, 32, 40\}$). Both main effects are significant (count: $p = 0.008$; BCS: $p = 0.0002$; $R^2 = 0.52$), but the interaction term is negligible (0.47\,pp). A TOST equivalence test with a $\pm 3$\,pp margin confirms that the interaction is bounded well below practical significance ($p_{\text{TOST}} = 0.003$); this supports bounded interaction between count and confusability effects.
230	
231	A $2 \times 2$ factorial sensitivity check on FewRel (count $\in \{8, 16\}$, BCS low/high; $n = 54$) was inconclusive ($p_{\text{TOST}} = 0.56$) due to limited power; this does not contradict the primary finding but limits generalization beyond the regression framework. The Few-NERD factorial (\S\ref{sec:multi_channel}) similarly did not reach significance ($p = 0.50$). The regression approach provides the stronger test by leveraging the full count range.
232	
233	\paragraph{Equivalence margin justification.}
234	We set the $\pm 3$\,pp margin based on three converging anchors. \emph{External}: evaluation variance in IE benchmarks typically spans 2--5\,pp across random seeds, prompt variations, and data splits \citep{card-2020-power,dror-2018-significance}; a within-margin interaction would be indistinguishable from routine evaluation noise. \emph{Internal}: $0.5 \times$ within-cell SD ($= 2.3$\,pp) corresponds to Cohen's $d \approx 0.5$ (medium effect threshold; \citealt{lakens-2017-equivalence}), cross-validated against the IQR-predicted confusability main effect of 2.2\,pp (\S\ref{sec:practical_magnitude})---an interaction exceeding 3\,pp would represent ${>}130\%$ amplification. \emph{Sensitivity}: Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} shows equivalence holds from $\pm 2$\,pp ($p = 0.047$) through $\pm 5$\,pp ($p < 0.001$) on the probability scale; the conclusion is not margin-dependent.
235	
236	\begin{table}[h]
237	\centering
238	\small
239	\begin{tabular}{crrl}
240	\toprule
241	\textbf{Margin (pp)} & \textbf{$t_{\text{upper}}$} & \textbf{$p_{\text{TOST}}$} & \textbf{Conclusion} \\
242	\midrule
243	$\pm 1$ & $-0.59$ & $0.278$ & Inconclusive \\
244	$\pm 2$ & $-1.70$ & $0.047$ & Equivalent \\
245	$\pm 3$ & $-2.81$ & $0.003$ & Equivalent \\
246	$\pm 5$ & $-5.02$ & ${<}\,0.001$ & Equivalent \\
247	\bottomrule
248	\end{tabular}
249	\caption{TOST sensitivity analysis across equivalence margins. The interaction effect (0.47\,pp, 90\% CI [$-$1.0, 2.0]\,pp) is bounded below 2\,pp at marginal significance ($p = 0.047$); the $\pm 3$\,pp choice does not drive the equivalence conclusion. At $\pm 1$\,pp, equivalence is inconclusive ($p = 0.278$), meaning interaction could reach approximately half the main effect magnitude.}
250	\label{tab:tost_sensitivity}
251	\end{table}
252	
253	\paragraph{Logit-scale sensitivity.}
254	Because accuracy near 50\% compresses interaction effects on the linear scale, we refit the regression on logit-transformed accuracy. The logit-scale interaction (0.111 logit units, $p = 0.047$, 90\% CI [0.019, 0.203]) is marginally significant, and TOST at the equivalent $\pm 3$\,pp margin ($\pm 0.156$ logit at median accuracy) is inconclusive ($p_{\text{TOST}} = 0.209$). However, back-transforming the interaction to the probability scale at the observed floor ($p = 0.51$) yields 2.78\,pp, still within the $\pm 3$\,pp margin; at the median baseline ($p = 0.65$), the back-transformed effect is 2.53\,pp. The logit TOST is inconclusive due to limited power ($n = 69$, wide CI) rather than a large effect size: the point estimate remains below the equivalence bound at all observed baselines. We retain the linear-scale TOST as the primary test and report this logit analysis as a sensitivity check. A regression-based TOST ($n = 375$, five count levels with continuous BCS) yields $p = 0.050$ at $\pm 3$\,pp and $p = 0.026$ at $\pm 3.5$\,pp on the logit scale, confirming the borderline status with substantially greater power than the factorial design.
255	
256	\paragraph{DocRED factorial (expanded).}
257	With expanded batches ($n{=}55$ per count level, up from $n{=}5$), the DocRED $2 \times 2$ factorial (count $\in \{16, 32\}$, BCS median split) yields an apparent interaction ($-$9.4\,pp, 95\% CI [$-$12.9, $-$6.0]): the BCS effect is $-$25.0\,pp at count 16 but $-$15.7\,pp at count 32. However, this attenuation is confounded by BCS range restriction: higher-count batches have narrower BCS ranges (spread 0.26 vs.\ 0.38), limiting achievable extremes.
258	
259	\paragraph{Range-matched robustness check.}
260	To control for the BCS range confound, we restrict both count levels to the overlapping BCS range [0.43, 0.70]. This retains 29 of 55 batches at count 16 (all 55 at count 32 fall within the range). Within this matched range, the BCS effect at count 16 shrinks from 15.95\,pp to 9.37\,pp (reflecting the narrower BCS span), while count 32 remains at 8.44\,pp. The interaction drops from 7.52\,pp (95\% CI [1.49, 12.83]) to 0.93\,pp (95\% CI [$-$7.13, 7.44])---the CI spans zero, consistent with no interaction. Spearman correlations remain comparable ($\rho = -0.65$ at count 16, $\rho = -0.69$ at count 32), confirming that the underlying BCS--accuracy relationship is robust across counts while the raw interaction magnitude was an artifact of differing achievable BCS ranges. This result aligns with the FewRel TOST equivalence finding and supports bounded association as the cross-dataset pattern. The reduced sample ($n = 29{+}55$) yields a minimum detectable effect of 5.5\,pp at 80\% power, consistent with insufficient power to distinguish a true null from a small ($<$5\,pp) interaction.
261	
262	\section{Within-Pool CI Details}
263	\label{sec:appendix_within_pool}
264	
265	The quartile $\Delta$ for 80-pool 16-relation subsets ($-$3.92\,pp, 95\% CI [$-$8.17, $+$0.38]) crosses zero, reflecting the compressed BCS range within that pool (0.46--0.68 vs.\ 0.48--0.71 for the independent pool). Within this narrow band, the quartile contrast lacks statistical power to detect the effect that the regression identifies. The full 80-relation condition ($n = 25$) is underpowered for both the slope (95\% CI [$-$1.303, $+$1.695]) and quartile analyses; we include it in Table~\ref{tab:within_pool} for completeness but do not draw inferential conclusions from it.
266	
267	\section{Controlling for Centrality Confounds in MAVEN Per-Type Analysis}
268	\label{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}
269	
270	Section~\ref{sec:centrality_confound} identifies a centrality confound in MAVEN's per-type BCS--F1 correlation: both embeddings show a \emph{positive} raw correlation, opposite to the expected direction. Here we formalize this analysis by identifying specific confounders for each embedding and testing whether the BCS--F1 relationship survives partial correlation controlling for these confounders.
271	
272	\paragraph{Confound identification.}
273	For each embedding, we computed the correlation between mean BCS and candidate confounders (type frequency, degree centrality at threshold 0.7) across MAVEN's 149 active event types. The dominant confound differs by embedding:
274	\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt,leftmargin=*]
275	\item \textbf{MiniLM}: BCS correlates with log type frequency ($r = 0.201$). Frequent types have more training signal and are extracted more reliably, which produces the positive BCS--F1 association.
276	\item \textbf{Qwen3}: BCS correlates strongly with degree centrality ($r = 0.854$). In the Qwen3 embedding space, high-BCS types are those with many semantic neighbors, central, well-represented types that the model extracts well.
277	\end{itemize}
278	
279	\paragraph{Partial correlation results.}
280	Table~\ref{tab:residualized_bcs} reports raw Spearman $\rho$ and partial Pearson $r$ after controlling for the identified confounders. Under single-variable control, both correlations weaken to non-significance. Under joint control (log frequency $+$ degree centrality simultaneously), neither embedding retains a significant BCS--F1 association.
281	
282	\begin{table}[h]
283	\centering
284	\small
285	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
286	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
287	\toprule
288	\textbf{Embedding} & \textbf{Control} & \textbf{Raw $\rho$} & \textbf{Raw $p$} & \textbf{Partial $r$} & \textbf{Partial $p$} \\
289	\midrule
290	MiniLM & --- & $+$0.208 & $0.011$ & --- & --- \\
291	MiniLM & log freq & --- & --- & $+$0.133 & $0.11$ \\
292	MiniLM & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.23$ \\
293	\midrule
294	Qwen3 & --- & $+$0.184 & $0.025$ & --- & --- \\
295	Qwen3 & degree centrality & --- & --- & $+$0.063 & $0.44$ \\
296	Qwen3 & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.44$ \\
297	\bottomrule
298	\end{tabular}}
299	\caption{MAVEN per-type BCS--F1 correlation before and after controlling for centrality confounders. Raw correlations are Spearman $\rho$; partial correlations are Pearson $r$ from residualized regression. Degree centrality is computed on the SCS graph at threshold 0.7. All values from experiment \texttt{exp\_maven\_residualized\_bcs}.}
300	\label{tab:residualized_bcs}
301	\end{table}
302	
303	\paragraph{Interpretation.}
304	The two embeddings encode different aspects of the same underlying confound. MiniLM, a smaller sentence encoder, produces a tighter embedding cluster for MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types; here, type frequency is the primary confounder because frequent types accumulate higher mean similarity to the dense cluster. Qwen3, with a larger representation space, differentiates types enough that frequency is not the dominant factor, but degree centrality (the count of strong semantic neighbors) becomes the confound instead. In both cases, the positive raw correlation reflects a centrality artifact: types that are semantically central are both high-BCS and easy to extract.
305	
306	Under joint control, both embeddings yield $p > 0.2$; the raw positive BCS--F1 correlation on MAVEN is thus fully attributable to centrality-related confounders. This does not invalidate the confusability hypothesis; the pairwise SCS--confusion correlation on MAVEN remains significant (MiniLM $r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\ref{tab:pairwise}). Rather, it demonstrates that per-type aggregation (mean BCS) conflates confusability with centrality in semantically homogeneous schemas, and that pairwise analysis or explicit confound adjustment is required in such settings.
307	
308	\subsection{Partial Correlation: Controlling for Degree Centrality}
309	\label{sec:appendix_partial_corr}
310	
311	To rule out degree centrality as a confound in cross-domain datasets, we compute partial correlations between BCS and accuracy controlling for batch mean degree centrality (mean off-diagonal SCS per type, averaged across batch members).
312	
313	\begin{table}[h]
314	\centering
315	\small
316	\begin{tabular}{lrrrrr}
317	\toprule
318	\textbf{Dataset} & \textbf{$n$} & \textbf{$r_{\text{zero}}$} & \textbf{$r_{\text{partial}}$} & \textbf{$p_{\text{partial}}$} & \textbf{VIF} \\
319	\midrule
320	FewRel & 204 & $-$0.570 & $-$0.537 & ${<}\,0.001$ & 1.18 \\
321	DocRED & 110 & $-$0.760 & $-$0.348 & ${<}\,0.001$ & 50.9 \\
322	\bottomrule
323	\end{tabular}
324	\caption{BCS--accuracy partial correlation controlling for degree centrality. FewRel shows minimal attenuation (VIF${}\,=\,1.18$, low collinearity). DocRED shows high BCS--centrality collinearity (VIF${}\,=\,50.9$, $r = 0.99$), but the partial correlation remains highly significant.}
325	\label{tab:partial_corr}
326	\end{table}
327	
328	As Table~\ref{tab:partial_corr} shows, on FewRel, BCS and degree centrality are only moderately correlated ($r = 0.39$), and the BCS effect survives nearly intact after partialling ($r_{\text{partial}} = -0.537$ vs.\ $r_{\text{zero}} = -0.570$; semipartial $R^2 = 0.273$). On DocRED, the near-perfect BCS--centrality correlation ($r = 0.99$) makes it impossible to fully separate the two predictors; the significant partial correlation ($p < 0.001$) indicates BCS retains predictive value but does not uniquely identify the causal channel. This contrasts with MAVEN (\S\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}), where the BCS signal vanishes after controlling for centrality.
329	
330	\subsection{NER BCS-Null Power Analysis}
331	\label{sec:appendix_ner_power}
332	
333	The Few-NERD BCS-null may reflect insufficient BCS variance rather than a genuine absence of the BCS--accuracy relationship. Few-NERD's BCS standard deviation (0.027) is only 19\% of FewRel's (0.141); FewRel's observed $r = -0.570$ would attenuate to $r = -0.111$ under this restricted range, yielding 65\% power at $n = 450$ (below the conventional 80\% threshold). The minimum detectable $|r|$ at 80\% power is 0.132. This analysis characterizes the NER result as absence of evidence (insufficient power at the attenuated effect size) rather than evidence of absence.

359	\section{Prompt Version Sensitivity Analysis}
360	\label{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}
361	
362	We assess whether the confusability effects reported in \S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}--\ref{sec:multi_channel} are robust to prompt design by replicating key experiments under the v2 prompt (\S\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).
363	
364	\paragraph{RE (FewRel).}
365	Under the v1 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap (Table~\ref{tab:bcs_controlled}) is substantial. Under v2, with $n{=}25$ batches per group and wider BCS contrast (low-BCS range [0.356, 0.367]; high-BCS range [0.791, 0.811]), the gap is $-$25.5\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\% vs.\ high-BCS 59.7\%; 95\% CI [22.4, 28.6]; $p < 0.001$; Pearson $r = -0.919$). The effect is significant, directionally consistent with v1, and only modestly attenuated in magnitude ($-$5.7\,pp reduction). An earlier $n{=}5$ evaluation with narrower BCS contrast (0.21) yielded a smaller gap of $-$7.9\,pp (95\% CI [$-$5.2, $+$21.5]), illustrating how limited statistical power and restricted BCS range can obscure the underlying signal, a cautionary note for small-sample prompt sensitivity studies.
366	
367	\paragraph{NER (Few-NERD).}
368	The batch-level BCS--F1 correlation is null under both prompt versions. Pooling across three seeds and all count levels ($n = 450$), the correlation is $r = 0.004$ ($p = 0.94$). An earlier small-sample analysis ($n = 27$, single seed) had reported positive correlations ($r = +0.50$/$+0.58$), but these were driven by an accidental BCS--type-frequency confound: those 27 batches exhibited $r(\text{BCS}, \text{freq}) = 0.53$, creating a spurious path through frequency to F1. After controlling for type frequency, the partial correlation is non-significant in both the small ($r = 0.28$, $p = 0.16$) and large ($r = -0.12$, $p = 0.29$) samples. The pairwise SCS--confusion analysis (\S\ref{sec:pairwise}), which is immune to this batch-level aggregation artifact, remains the primary evidence for the NER confusability dimension.
369	
370	\paragraph{Interpretation.}
371	The $n{=}25$ v2 result confirms that the confusability effect is directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude: the gap narrows ($-$5.7\,pp) but remains highly significant ($p < 0.001$, $r = -0.919$). The earlier $n{=}5$ non-significance reflected insufficient power and narrow BCS contrast, not absence of the effect. V2's stricter format constraints raise the baseline accuracy (low-BCS 85.2\% vs.\ 82.3\%), but type confusion under high confusability persists (59.7\% vs.\ 51.0\%). Prompt design modulates the practical magnitude of the confusability effect but does not eliminate it, ruling out prompt-specific confounding as the primary source. The underlying confusability signal (pairwise SCS--confusion correlation and the directional BCS--accuracy relationship) persists across both prompt versions.
372	
373	\subsection{Cross-Prompt-Variant BCS Correlation}
374	\label{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}
375	
376	To verify that the BCS--accuracy relationship is not an artifact of a specific prompt template, we evaluate four structurally distinct prompt variants on FewRel 80-relation RE at two count levels (8 and 32), with 10 random batches per condition (Table~\ref{tab:prompt_variants}).
377	
378	\begin{table}[h]
379	\centering
380	\small
381	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{\begin{tabular}{llrrrr}
382	\toprule
383	\textbf{Variant} & \textbf{Count} & \textbf{Mean Acc} & \textbf{$r$} & \textbf{$p$} \\
384	\midrule
385	V2 (definition-first) & 8 & 0.769 & $-$0.333 & 0.348 \\
386	V2 (definition-first) & 32 & 0.564 & $-$0.648 & 0.043 \\
387	Instruction-rewrite & 8 & 0.847 & $-$0.223 & 0.536 \\
388	Instruction-rewrite & 32 & 0.662 & $-$0.469 & 0.171 \\
389	Chain-of-thought & 8 & 0.749 & $+$0.254 & 0.478 \\
390	Chain-of-thought & 32 & 0.617 & $-$0.566 & 0.088 \\
391	Minimal & 8 & 0.852 & $-$0.170 & 0.638 \\
392	Minimal & 32 & 0.615 & $-$0.771 & 0.009 \\
393	\bottomrule
394	\end{tabular}}
395	\caption{BCS--accuracy Pearson correlation across four prompt variants. At count 32, all four variants show negative BCS--accuracy correlations ($r \leq -0.47$), with two reaching $p < 0.05$. At count 8, the narrower BCS range reduces statistical power.}
396	\label{tab:prompt_variants}
397	\end{table}
398	
399	At count 32, all four variants produce negative BCS--accuracy correlations ($r$ ranges from $-$0.47 to $-$0.77), with V2 ($p = 0.043$) and Minimal ($p = 0.009$) reaching significance and Chain-of-thought approaching it ($p = 0.088$). At count 8, correlations are weaker and nonsignificant across all variants, consistent with the narrower BCS range at lower schema complexity reducing the detectable effect size. Rather than applying Bonferroni correction, which assumes independent hypotheses inappropriate for correlated prompt variants testing the same underlying signal, we note directional consistency: 7 of 8 conditions show negative BCS--accuracy slopes, consistent with the primary finding \citep{rothman-1990}. These results confirm that the confusability effect arises from schema composition, not prompt-specific phrasing.
400	
401	\section{BCS Aggregation Sensitivity}
402	\label{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}
403	
404	We define Batch Confusability Score (BCS) as the \emph{mean} pairwise SCS across all type pairs in a batch (\S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}). An alternative definition takes the \emph{maximum} pairwise SCS within each batch to capture the single most confusable pair. Here we compare both aggregation methods to verify that core conclusions are robust to this choice (Table~\ref{tab:bcs_aggregation}).
405	
406	\begin{table}[h]
407	\centering
408	\small
409	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
410	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
411	\toprule
412	\textbf{Aggregation} & \textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Slope} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{$r$} & \textbf{$p$} \\
413	\midrule
414	MEAN (primary) & 16-rel & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}\,0.001$ \\
415	MEAN & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\
416	\midrule
417	MAX & 16-rel & $-$0.449 & [$-$0.552, $-$0.346] & $-$0.637 & ${<}\,0.001$ \\
418	MAX & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\
419	\bottomrule
420	\end{tabular}}
421	\caption{BCS--accuracy regression under mean vs.\ max pairwise SCS aggregation (FewRel, 16-relation batches). Permutation test for slope equivalence across pools: MEAN $p \approx 0.60$; MAX $p \approx 0.44$. The 80-pool row is identical under both definitions because these batches were collected using mean-aggregated BCS; the MAX column reflects the same data for completeness.}
422	\label{tab:bcs_aggregation}
423	\end{table}
424	
425	MAX aggregation yields stronger correlations ($r = -0.637$ vs.\ $-0.467$ for 16-rel), consistent with the hypothesis that the single most confusable pair in a batch predicts substitution errors. Both definitions produce significant negative slopes across all conditions, and the permutation tests confirm no significant difference between independent and sampled pools under either definition. We adopt MEAN as the primary definition because it better represents average pairwise difficulty across all schema combinations, while MAX may overweight a single outlier pair. The choice does not affect any paper conclusion.
426	
427	\section{BCS-Controlled Detailed Results}
428	\label{app:bcs-controlled-table}
429	
430	\begin{table}[h]
431	\centering
432	\small
433	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
434	\begin{tabular}{lcrrrr}
435	\toprule
436	\textbf{Schema} & \textbf{Count} & \textbf{Low-BCS} & \textbf{High-BCS} & \textbf{$\Delta$ (pp)} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
437	\midrule
438	16-rel & 8 & 88.9\% & 79.8\% & $-$9.1 & --- \\
439	80-rel & 8 & 82.3\% & 51.0\% & $-$31.2 & {[}17.7, 42.7{]} \\
440	80-rel & 16 & 82.8\% & 51.6\% & $-$31.2 & {[}22.9, 39.6{]} \\
441	\bottomrule
442	\end{tabular}}
443	\caption{BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. Low-BCS batches contain maximally dissimilar types; high-BCS batches contain maximally similar types. The 80-relation extreme-batch gap reflects the wider BCS range; regression slopes are consistent across pools ($p = 0.60$; Table~\ref{tab:within_pool}; see \S\ref{sec:practical_magnitude} for typical-batch magnitudes). The 16-relation condition used a single extreme batch per BCS level; bootstrap CIs require multiple batches.}
444	\label{tab:bcs_controlled}
445	\end{table}
446	
447	\paragraph{Adjusted $R^2$ and confidence intervals.}
448	DocRED regressions (\S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) use OLS on $n = 55$ batches (15 high-BCS + 15 low-BCS + 25 random) per count level, with one predictor. At count 16, adjusted $R^2 = 0.646$ (unadjusted: 0.653; 95\% CI [0.53, 0.77]); at count 32, adjusted $R^2 = 0.606$ (unadjusted: 0.614; 95\% CI [0.47, 0.75]). The small shrinkage ($\Delta R^2 \leq 0.01$) indicates the unadjusted values are not inflated by the predictor-to-sample ratio.
449	
450	\section{Format Independence: Paired Constrained vs.\ Unconstrained Comparison}
451	\label{app:format-independence}
452	
453	To test whether the BCS--accuracy relationship depends on the output format, we run both constrained (enum-based) and unconstrained (free-text) decoding on the same 25 batch type sets from the FewRel 80-relation pool (13 low-BCS, 12 high-BCS; count 8). Each batch uses identical instances and type sets; only the decoding format differs (Table~\ref{tab:format_independence}).
454	
455	\begin{table}[h]
456	\centering
457	\small
458	\begin{tabular}{lrr}
459	\toprule
460	\textbf{Metric} & \textbf{Constrained} & \textbf{Unconstrained} \\
461	\midrule
462	Mean accuracy & 79.5\% & 79.6\% \\
463	Pearson $r$ (BCS vs.\ acc) & $-$0.448 ($p = 0.025$) & $-$0.439 ($p = 0.028$) \\
464	Spearman $\rho$ (BCS vs.\ acc) & $-$0.157 ($p = 0.455$) & $-$0.144 ($p = 0.492$) \\
465	\midrule
466	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\textit{Paired Fisher $z$-test for correlation equality}} \\
467	\quad Pearson $z$ & \multicolumn{2}{c}{$-$0.04 ($p = 0.97$)} \\
468	\quad Spearman $z$ & \multicolumn{2}{c}{$-$0.04 ($p = 0.97$)} \\
469	\bottomrule
470	\end{tabular}
471	\caption{Paired comparison of constrained vs.\ unconstrained decoding on 25 identical FewRel batch type sets. Both formats yield virtually identical BCS--accuracy correlations (Fisher $z \approx 0$), confirming the confusability effect is format-independent. Mean accuracy is also unchanged ($\Delta = -0.08$\,pp), ruling out a format-driven accuracy shift.}
472	\label{tab:format_independence}
473	\end{table}
474	
475	The near-zero Fisher $z$ statistics ($|z| < 0.05$, $p > 0.96$) confirm that the BCS--accuracy correlation is indistinguishable between formats. Both Pearson $r$ values are significant ($p < 0.03$), reflecting the bimodal BCS distribution of extreme batches (13 low + 12 high); the monotonic Spearman $\rho$ is modest and non-significant under both formats, consistent with the typical-batch BCS effect size (${\sim}2$\,pp; \S\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}). This result establishes that the confusability-associated degradation pattern is robust across output formats, not an artifact of forced-choice decoding.
476	
477	\paragraph{Per-batch paired results.}
478	Table~\ref{tab:format_perbatch} reports per-batch accuracy under both formats. Of 25 batches, 23 yield \emph{identical} accuracy; the two exceptions (high\_5, high\_11) differ by a single sample (1/96 $\approx$ 1.04\,pp). Only 2 out-of-vocabulary predictions occur in unconstrained decoding (OOV rate 0.08\%), confirming that the model generates valid type labels even without format constraints.

DocRE missed rates decrease with BCS, indicating high-confusability errors shift from omission to substitution.}\n\\label{tab:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & $n$ & $\\rho$ & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-32B & 154 & 0.954 & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nGemini 2.5 Flash & 60 & 0.821 & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & 54 & 0.430 & 0.001 \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-70B$^\\dagger$ & 20 & $-$0.086 & 0.719 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cross-model replication of IntraSub--BCS Spearman correlation on FewRel RE. $^\\dagger$LLaMA-70B has only 20 batches with 17.4\\% missed rate; the null is attributable to insufficient power rather than absence of the effect.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model_error_taxonomy}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{SDGD Full Protocol and Results}\n\\label{sec:appendix_sdgd}\n\nWe construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \\tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\\textsc{largest\\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent (i.e., highly confusable) types share a color. Each color class forms one extraction batch, and predictions are merged via set union.\n\n\\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]\n\\item Compute pairwise SCS matrix $S_{ij} = \\cos(\\text{embed}(t_i), \\text{embed}(t_j))$.\n\\item Construct graph $G$: add edge $(t_i, t_j)$ iff $S_{ij} > \\tau$.\n\\item Apply greedy graph coloring (\\textsc{largest\\_first}) to assign each type a color $c(t)$.\n\\item For each color $k$, form batch $B_k = \\{t : c(t) = k\\}$ and run extraction on all test instances.\n\\item Merge: union of per-batch predictions (v1) or confidence-gated selection (v2).\n\\end{enumerate}\nOracle routing (Table~\\ref{tab:routing_strategies}) recovers substantial accuracy (72.4\\% vs.\\ 50.4\\% single-pass), confirming the sub-schemas contain correct predictions; the failure lies in routing, not decomposition quality.\n\nWe compare four modes: (i)~\\textbf{single\\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\\textbf{sdgd\\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\\textbf{random\\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\\textbf{per\\_type}, the extreme case where each type is extracted in isolation.\n\nSingle-pass extraction achieves F1\\,=\\,0.445 (P\\,=\\,0.563, R\\,=\\,0.368). SDGD obtains F1\\,=\\,0.251 (P\\,=\\,0.210, R\\,=\\,0.314), a 44\\% relative drop. Random batching reaches F1\\,=\\,0.320 (P\\,=\\,0.249, R\\,=\\,0.446), and per-type extraction yields F1\\,=\\,0.292 (P\\,=\\,0.194, R\\,=\\,0.586). Every decomposition mode falls below the single-pass baseline (Table~\\ref{tab:sdgd_results}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Mode} & \\textbf{\\# Batches} & \\textbf{Precision} & \\textbf{Recall} & \\textbf{F1} \\\\\n\\midrule\nsingle\\_pass & 1 & 0.563 & 0.368 & 0.445 \\\\\n\\midrule\nsdgd\\_zero (v1) & 8 & 0.210 & 0.314 & 0.251 \\\\\nrandom\\_batch (v1) & 8 & 0.249 & 0.446 & 0.320 \\\\\nper\\_type & 66 & 0.194 & 0.586 & 0.292 \\\\\n\\midrule\nsdgd\\_zero (v2) & 4 & 0.324 & 0.191 & 0.241 \\\\\nrandom\\_batch (v2) & 8 & 0.331 & 0.276 & 0.301 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Few-NERD extraction under decomposition modes. V1 uses union merge; v2 uses confidence-gated merge with \\texttt{min\\_batch\\_size=8} (reducing 8$\\to$4 batches for sdgd\\_zero). All decomposition modes produce lower F1 than the single-pass baseline. All modes use the v1 prompt.}\n\\label{tab:sdgd_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nDecomposition boosts recall (per-type recall reaches 0.586 versus single-pass 0.368) but devastates precision (0.194 versus 0.563). When the same text is presented to the model $k$ times with different type subsets, each pass independently generates entity predictions. Because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes, the union merge step accumulates overlapping and contradictory predictions and inflates false positives.\n\nOur v2 merge ranks competing predictions by the LLM's self-reported confidence and retains only the highest-scoring candidate for each span. Confidence gating improves precision (sdgd\\_zero: 0.210$\\to$0.324; random\\_batch: 0.249$\\to$0.331), but at the cost of disproportionate recall loss (0.314$\\to$0.191; 0.446$\\to$0.276), and both modes produce lower F1 (sdgd\\_zero: 0.251$\\to$0.241; random\\_batch: 0.320$\\to$0.301).\n\n\\subsection{Practical Routing Strategies on RE}\n\\label{sec:appendix_routing}\n\nWe evaluate five non-oracle routing strategies on FewRel 80-relation RE using graph-coloring sub-schema decomposition (same grouping as Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition}). Each strategy selects a single sub-schema for each test instance and returns that sub-schema's prediction.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Strategy} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ vs.\\ Single-Pass} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOracle (gold label) & 72.4\\% & +22.0\\,pp \\\\\n\\midrule\nMax-conf (positive only) & 36.5\\% & $-$14.0\\,pp \\\\\nNone-aware & 36.5\\% & $-$14.0\\,pp \\\\\nMargin (positive only) & 35.9\\% & $-$14.5\\,pp \\\\\nMax-conf (all) & 27.7\\% & $-$22.7\\,pp \\\\\nRandom & 14.7\\% & $-$35.7\\,pp \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-pass (no decomposition) & 50.4\\% & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Routing strategy comparison on FewRel 80-relation RE with graph-coloring sub-schema decomposition. Oracle requires gold labels. All practical (non-oracle) strategies perform below the single-pass baseline. The failure stems from constrained decoding (enum constraint), which forces confident predictions in every sub-schema: 73.4\\% of instances receive positive predictions from 3--5 sub-schemas simultaneously, with per-sub-schema none-rates of only 20--40\\%.}\n\\label{tab:routing_strategies}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Factorial Analysis and Separability Test}\n\\label{sec:appendix_factorial}\n\nTo formally test whether count and confusability effects are additive, we fit a multiple regression (accuracy $\\sim$ $\\log_2$\\,count $+$ BCS $+$ count$\\times$BCS) across 69 batches from FewRel spanning five count levels ($k \\in \\{4, 8, 16, 32, 40\\}$). Both main effects are significant (count: $p = 0.008$; BCS: $p = 0.0002$; $R^2 = 0.52$), but the interaction term is negligible (0.47\\,pp). A TOST equivalence test with a $\\pm 3$\\,pp margin confirms that the interaction is bounded well below practical significance ($p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.003$); this supports bounded interaction between count and confusability effects.\n\nA $2 \\times 2$ factorial sensitivity check on FewRel (count $\\in \\{8, 16\\}$, BCS low/high; $n = 54$) was inconclusive ($p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.56$) due to limited power; this does not contradict the primary finding but limits generalization beyond the regression framework. The Few-NERD factorial (\\S\\ref{sec:multi_channel}) similarly did not reach significance ($p = 0.50$). The regression approach provides the stronger test by leveraging the full count range.\n\n\\paragraph{Equivalence margin justification.}\nWe set the $\\pm 3$\\,pp margin based on three converging anchors. \\emph{External}: evaluation variance in IE benchmarks typically spans 2--5\\,pp across random seeds, prompt variations, and data splits \\citep{card-2020-power,dror-2018-significance}; a within-margin interaction would be indistinguishable from routine evaluation noise. \\emph{Internal}: $0.5 \\times$ within-cell SD ($= 2.3$\\,pp) corresponds to Cohen's $d \\approx 0.5$ (medium effect threshold; \\citealt{lakens-2017-equivalence}), cross-validated against the IQR-predicted confusability main effect of 2.2\\,pp (\\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude})---an interaction exceeding 3\\,pp would represent ${>}130\\%$ amplification. \\emph{Sensitivity}: Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} shows equivalence holds from $\\pm 2$\\,pp ($p = 0.047$) through $\\pm 5$\\,pp ($p < 0.001$) on the probability scale; the conclusion is not margin-dependent.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{crrl}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Margin (pp)} & \\textbf{$t_{\\text{upper}}$} & \\textbf{$p_{\\text{TOST}}$} & \\textbf{Conclusion} \\\\\n\\midrule\n$\\pm 1$ & $-0.59$ & $0.278$ & Inconclusive \\\\\n$\\pm 2$ & $-1.70$ & $0.047$ & Equivalent \\\\\n$\\pm 3$ & $-2.81$ & $0.003$ & Equivalent \\\\\n$\\pm 5$ & $-5.02$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ & Equivalent \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{TOST sensitivity analysis across equivalence margins. The interaction effect (0.47\\,pp, 90\\% CI [$-$1.0, 2.0]\\,pp) is bounded below 2\\,pp at marginal significance ($p = 0.047$); the $\\pm 3$\\,pp choice does not drive the equivalence conclusion. At $\\pm 1$\\,pp, equivalence is inconclusive ($p = 0.278$), meaning interaction could reach approximately half the main effect magnitude.}\n\\label{tab:tost_sensitivity}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Logit-scale sensitivity.}\nBecause accuracy near 50\\% compresses interaction effects on the linear scale, we refit the regression on logit-transformed accuracy. The logit-scale interaction (0.111 logit units, $p = 0.047$, 90\\% CI [0.019, 0.203]) is marginally significant, and TOST at the equivalent $\\pm 3$\\,pp margin ($\\pm 0.156$ logit at median accuracy) is inconclusive ($p_{\\text{TOST}} = 0.209$). However, back-transforming the interaction to the probability scale at the observed floor ($p = 0.51$) yields 2.78\\,pp, still within the $\\pm 3$\\,pp margin; at the median baseline ($p = 0.65$), the back-transformed effect is 2.53\\,pp. The logit TOST is inconclusive due to limited power ($n = 69$, wide CI) rather than a large effect size: the point estimate remains below the equivalence bound at all observed baselines. We retain the linear-scale TOST as the primary test and report this logit analysis as a sensitivity check. A regression-based TOST ($n = 375$, five count levels with continuous BCS) yields $p = 0.050$ at $\\pm 3$\\,pp and $p = 0.026$ at $\\pm 3.5$\\,pp on the logit scale, confirming the borderline status with substantially greater power than the factorial design.\n\n\\paragraph{DocRED factorial (expanded).}\nWith expanded batches ($n{=}55$ per count level, up from $n{=}5$), the DocRED $2 \\times 2$ factorial (count $\\in \\{16, 32\\}$, BCS median split) yields an apparent interaction ($-$9.4\\,pp, 95\\% CI [$-$12.9, $-$6.0]): the BCS effect is $-$25.0\\,pp at count 16 but $-$15.7\\,pp at count 32. However, this attenuation is confounded by BCS range restriction: higher-count batches have narrower BCS ranges (spread 0.26 vs.\\ 0.38), limiting achievable extremes.\n\n\\paragraph{Range-matched robustness check.}\nTo control for the BCS range confound, we restrict both count levels to the overlapping BCS range [0.43, 0.70]. This retains 29 of 55 batches at count 16 (all 55 at count 32 fall within the range). Within this matched range, the BCS effect at count 16 shrinks from 15.95\\,pp to 9.37\\,pp (reflecting the narrower BCS span), while count 32 remains at 8.44\\,pp. The interaction drops from 7.52\\,pp (95\\% CI [1.49, 12.83]) to 0.93\\,pp (95\\% CI [$-$7.13, 7.44])---the CI spans zero, consistent with no interaction. Spearman correlations remain comparable ($\\rho = -0.65$ at count 16, $\\rho = -0.69$ at count 32), confirming that the underlying BCS--accuracy relationship is robust across counts while the raw interaction magnitude was an artifact of differing achievable BCS ranges. This result aligns with the FewRel TOST equivalence finding and supports bounded association as the cross-dataset pattern. The reduced sample ($n = 29{+}55$) yields a minimum detectable effect of 5.5\\,pp at 80\\% power, consistent with insufficient power to distinguish a true null from a small ($<$5\\,pp) interaction.\n\n\\section{Within-Pool CI Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_within_pool}\n\nThe quartile $\\Delta$ for 80-pool 16-relation subsets ($-$3.92\\,pp, 95\\% CI [$-$8.17, $+$0.38]) crosses zero, reflecting the compressed BCS range within that pool (0.46--0.68 vs.\\ 0.48--0.71 for the independent pool). Within this narrow band, the quartile contrast lacks statistical power to detect the effect that the regression identifies. The full 80-relation condition ($n = 25$) is underpowered for both the slope (95\\% CI [$-$1.303, $+$1.695]) and quartile analyses; we include it in Table~\\ref{tab:within_pool} for completeness but do not draw inferential conclusions from it.\n\n\\section{Controlling for Centrality Confounds in MAVEN Per-Type Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:centrality_confound} identifies a centrality confound in MAVEN's per-type BCS--F1 correlation: both embeddings show a \\emph{positive} raw correlation, opposite to the expected direction. Here we formalize this analysis by identifying specific confounders for each embedding and testing whether the BCS--F1 relationship survives partial correlation controlling for these confounders.\n\n\\paragraph{Confound identification.}\nFor each embedding, we computed the correlation between mean BCS and candidate confounders (type frequency, degree centrality at threshold 0.7) across MAVEN's 149 active event types. The dominant confound differs by embedding:\n\\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{MiniLM}: BCS correlates with log type frequency ($r = 0.201$). Frequent types have more training signal and are extracted more reliably, which produces the positive BCS--F1 association.\n\\item \\textbf{Qwen3}: BCS correlates strongly with degree centrality ($r = 0.854$). In the Qwen3 embedding space, high-BCS types are those with many semantic neighbors, central, well-represented types that the model extracts well.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{Partial correlation results.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:residualized_bcs} reports raw Spearman $\\rho$ and partial Pearson $r$ after controlling for the identified confounders. Under single-variable control, both correlations weaken to non-significance. Under joint control (log frequency $+$ degree centrality simultaneously), neither embedding retains a significant BCS--F1 association.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Embedding} & \\textbf{Control} & \\textbf{Raw $\\rho$} & \\textbf{Raw $p$} & \\textbf{Partial $r$} & \\textbf{Partial $p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMiniLM & --- & $+$0.208 & $0.011$ & --- & --- \\\\\nMiniLM & log freq & --- & --- & $+$0.133 & $0.11$ \\\\\nMiniLM & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.23$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 & --- & $+$0.184 & $0.025$ & --- & --- \\\\\nQwen3 & degree centrality & --- & --- & $+$0.063 & $0.44$ \\\\\nQwen3 & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.44$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{MAVEN per-type BCS--F1 correlation before and after controlling for centrality confounders. Raw correlations are Spearman $\\rho$; partial correlations are Pearson $r$ from residualized regression. Degree centrality is computed on the SCS graph at threshold 0.7. All values from experiment \\texttt{exp\\_maven\\_residualized\\_bcs}.}\n\\label{tab:residualized_bcs}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThe two embeddings encode different aspects of the same underlying confound. MiniLM, a smaller sentence encoder, produces a tighter embedding cluster for MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types; here, type frequency is the primary confounder because frequent types accumulate higher mean similarity to the dense cluster. Qwen3, with a larger representation space, differentiates types enough that frequency is not the dominant factor, but degree centrality (the count of strong semantic neighbors) becomes the confound instead. In both cases, the positive raw correlation reflects a centrality artifact: types that are semantically central are both high-BCS and easy to extract.\n\nUnder joint control, both embeddings yield $p > 0.2$; the raw positive BCS--F1 correlation on MAVEN is thus fully attributable to centrality-related confounders. This does not invalidate the confusability hypothesis; the pairwise SCS--confusion correlation on MAVEN remains significant (MiniLM $r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}). Rather, it demonstrates that per-type aggregation (mean BCS) conflates confusability with centrality in semantically homogeneous schemas, and that pairwise analysis or explicit confound adjustment is required in such settings.\n\n\\subsection{Partial Correlation: Controlling for Degree Centrality}\n\\label{sec:appendix_partial_corr}\n\nTo rule out degree centrality as a confound in cross-domain datasets, we compute partial correlations between BCS and accuracy controlling for batch mean degree centrality (mean off-diagonal SCS per type, averaged across batch members).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{$n$} & \\textbf{$r_{\\text{zero}}$} & \\textbf{$r_{\\text{partial}}$} & \\textbf{$p_{\\text{partial}}$} & \\textbf{VIF} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFewRel & 204 & $-$0.570 & $-$0.537 & ${<}\\,0.001$ & 1.18 \\\\\nDocRED & 110 & $-$0.760 & $-$0.348 & ${<}\\,0.001$ & 50.9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{BCS--accuracy partial correlation controlling for degree centrality. FewRel shows minimal attenuation (VIF${}\\,=\\,1.18$, low collinearity). DocRED shows high BCS--centrality collinearity (VIF${}\\,=\\,50.9$, $r = 0.99$), but the partial correlation remains highly significant.}\n\\label{tab:partial_corr}\n\\end{table}\n\nAs Table~\\ref{tab:partial_corr} shows, on FewRel, BCS and degree centrality are only moderately correlated ($r = 0.39$), and the BCS effect survives nearly intact after partialling ($r_{\\text{partial}} = -0.537$ vs.\\ $r_{\\text{zero}} = -0.570$; semipartial $R^2 = 0.273$). On DocRED, the near-perfect BCS--centrality correlation ($r = 0.99$) makes it impossible to fully separate the two predictors; the significant partial correlation ($p < 0.001$) indicates BCS retains predictive value but does not uniquely identify the causal channel. This contrasts with MAVEN (\\S\\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}), where the BCS signal vanishes after controlling for centrality.\n\n\\subsection{NER BCS-Null Power Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_ner_power}\n\nThe Few-NERD BCS-null may reflect insufficient BCS variance rather than a genuine absence of the BCS--accuracy relationship. Few-NERD's BCS standard deviation (0.027) is only 19\\% of FewRel's (0.141); FewRel's observed $r = -0.570$ would attenuate to $r = -0.111$ under this restricted range, yielding 65\\% power at $n = 450$ (below the conventional 80\\% threshold). The minimum detectable $|r|$ at 80\\% power is 0.132. This analysis characterizes the NER result as absence of evidence (insufficient power at the attenuated effect size) rather than evidence of absence.\n\n\\section{Batch Composition Results}\n\\label{sec:appendix_batch_composition}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{Mean BCS} \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-pass (80 types) & 0.504 & 0.557 \\\\\nRandom batching (15 seeds)$^\\dagger$ & 0.788\\,$\\pm$\\,0.018 & 0.559 \\\\\nConfus.-aware (graph coloring) & \\textbf{0.802} & 0.552 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Batch composition on FewRel 80-relation RE with oracle routing. $^\\dagger$Mean $\\pm$ SD across 15 random seeds.}\n\\label{tab:batch_composition}\n\\end{table}\n\nSee the accompanying discussion in \\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}.\n\n\\input{prompt_appendix}\n\n\\section{Prompt Version Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}\n\nWe assess whether the confusability effects reported in \\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}--\\ref{sec:multi_channel} are robust to prompt design by replicating key experiments under the v2 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).\n\n\\paragraph{RE (FewRel).}\nUnder the v1 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap (Table~\\ref{tab:bcs_controlled}) is substantial. Under v2, with $n{=}25$ batches per group and wider BCS contrast (low-BCS range [0.356, 0.367]; high-BCS range [0.791, 0.811]), the gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; 95\\% CI [22.4, 28.6]; $p < 0.001$; Pearson $r = -0.919$). The effect is significant, directionally consistent with v1, and only modestly attenuated in magnitude ($-$5.7\\,pp reduction). An earlier $n{=}5$ evaluation with narrower BCS contrast (0.21) yielded a smaller gap of $-$7.9\\,pp (95\\% CI [$-$5.2, $+$21.5]), illustrating how limited statistical power and restricted BCS range can obscure the underlying signal, a cautionary note for small-sample prompt sensitivity studies.\n\n\\paragraph{NER (Few-NERD).}\nThe batch-level BCS--F1 correlation is null under both prompt versions. Pooling across three seeds and all count levels ($n = 450$), the correlation is $r = 0.004$ ($p = 0.94$). An earlier small-sample analysis ($n = 27$, single seed) had reported positive correlations ($r = +0.50$/$+0.58$), but these were driven by an accidental BCS--type-frequency confound: those 27 batches exhibited $r(\\text{BCS}, \\text{freq}) = 0.53$, creating a spurious path through frequency to F1. After controlling for type frequency, the partial correlation is non-significant in both the small ($r = 0.28$, $p = 0.16$) and large ($r = -0.12$, $p = 0.29$) samples. The pairwise SCS--confusion analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}), which is immune to this batch-level aggregation artifact, remains the primary evidence for the NER confusability dimension.\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThe $n{=}25$ v2 result confirms that the confusability effect is directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude: the gap narrows ($-$5.7\\,pp) but remains highly significant ($p < 0.001$, $r = -0.919$). The earlier $n{=}5$ non-significance reflected insufficient power and narrow BCS contrast, not absence of the effect. V2's stricter format constraints raise the baseline accuracy (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ 82.3\\%), but type confusion under high confusability persists (59.7\\% vs.\\ 51.0\\%). Prompt design modulates the practical magnitude of the confusability effect but does not eliminate it, ruling out prompt-specific confounding as the primary source. The underlying confusability signal (pairwise SCS--confusion correlation and the directional BCS--accuracy relationship) persists across both prompt versions.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Prompt-Variant BCS Correlation}\n\\label{sec:appendix_prompt_variants}\n\nTo verify that the BCS--accuracy relationship is not an artifact of a specific prompt template, we evaluate four structurally distinct prompt variants on FewRel 80-relation RE at two count levels (8 and 32), with 10 random batches per condition (Table~\\ref{tab:prompt_variants}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{llrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & \\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Mean Acc} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nV2 (definition-first) & 8 & 0.769 & $-$0.333 & 0.348 \\\\\nV2 (definition-first) & 32 & 0.564 & $-$0.648 & 0.043 \\\\\nInstruction-rewrite & 8 & 0.847 & $-$0.223 & 0.536 \\\\\nInstruction-rewrite & 32 & 0.662 & $-$0.469 & 0.171 \\\\\nChain-of-thought & 8 & 0.749 & $+$0.254 & 0.478 \\\\\nChain-of-thought & 32 & 0.617 & $-$0.566 & 0.088 \\\\\nMinimal & 8 & 0.852 & $-$0.170 & 0.638 \\\\\nMinimal & 32 & 0.615 & $-$0.771 & 0.009 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{BCS--accuracy Pearson correlation across four prompt variants. At count 32, all four variants show negative BCS--accuracy correlations ($r \\leq -0.47$), with two reaching $p < 0.05$. At count 8, the narrower BCS range reduces statistical power.}\n\\label{tab:prompt_variants}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt count 32, all four variants produce negative BCS--accuracy correlations ($r$ ranges from $-$0.47 to $-$0.77), with V2 ($p = 0.043$) and Minimal ($p = 0.009$) reaching significance and Chain-of-thought approaching it ($p = 0.088$). At count 8, correlations are weaker and nonsignificant across all variants, consistent with the narrower BCS range at lower schema complexity reducing the detectable effect size. Rather than applying Bonferroni correction, which assumes independent hypotheses inappropriate for correlated prompt variants testing the same underlying signal, we note directional consistency: 7 of 8 conditions show negative BCS--accuracy slopes, consistent with the primary finding \\citep{rothman-1990}. These results confirm that the confusability effect arises from schema composition, not prompt-specific phrasing.\n\n\\section{BCS Aggregation Sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}\n\nWe define Batch Confusability Score (BCS) as the \\emph{mean} pairwise SCS across all type pairs in a batch (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}). An alternative definition takes the \\emph{maximum} pairwise SCS within each batch to capture the single most confusable pair. Here we compare both aggregation methods to verify that core conclusions are robust to this choice (Table~\\ref{tab:bcs_aggregation}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Aggregation} & \\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Slope} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMEAN (primary) & 16-rel & $-$0.509 & [$-$0.686, $-$0.336] & $-$0.467 & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMEAN & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMAX & 16-rel & $-$0.449 & [$-$0.552, $-$0.346] & $-$0.637 & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMAX & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{BCS--accuracy regression under mean vs.\\ max pairwise SCS aggregation (FewRel, 16-relation batches). Permutation test for slope equivalence across pools: MEAN $p \\approx 0.60$; MAX $p \\approx 0.44$. The 80-pool row is identical under both definitions because these batches were collected using mean-aggregated BCS; the MAX column reflects the same data for completeness.}\n\\label{tab:bcs_aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\nMAX aggregation yields stronger correlations ($r = -0.637$ vs.\\ $-0.467$ for 16-rel), consistent with the hypothesis that the single most confusable pair in a batch predicts substitution errors. Both definitions produce significant negative slopes across all conditions, and the permutation tests confirm no significant difference between independent and sampled pools under either definition. We adopt MEAN as the primary definition because it better represents average pairwise difficulty across all schema combinations, while MAX may overweight a single outlier pair. The choice does not affect any paper conclusion.\n\n\\section{BCS-Controlled Detailed Results}\n\\label{app:bcs-controlled-table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Schema} & \\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Low-BCS} & \\textbf{High-BCS} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\n16-rel & 8 & 88.9\\% & 79.8\\% & $-$9.1 & --- \\\\\n80-rel & 8 & 82.3\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$31.2 & {[}17.7, 42.7{]} \\\\\n80-rel & 16 & 82.8\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$31.2 & {[}22.9, 39.6{]} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. Low-BCS batches contain maximally dissimilar types; high-BCS batches contain maximally similar types. The 80-relation extreme-batch gap reflects the wider BCS range; regression slopes are consistent across pools ($p = 0.60$; Table~\\ref{tab:within_pool}; see \\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude} for typical-batch magnitudes). The 16-relation condition used a single extreme batch per BCS level; bootstrap CIs require multiple batches.}\n\\label{tab:bcs_controlled}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Adjusted $R^2$ and confidence intervals.}\nDocRED regressions (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) use OLS on $n = 55$ batches (15 high-BCS + 15 low-BCS + 25 random) per count level, with one predictor. At count 16, adjusted $R^2 = 0.646$ (unadjusted: 0.653; 95\\% CI [0.53, 0.77]); at count 32, adjusted $R^2 = 0.606$ (unadjusted: 0.614; 95\\% CI [0.47, 0.75]). The small shrinkage ($\\Delta R^2 \\leq 0.01$) indicates the unadjusted values are not inflated by the predictor-to-sample ratio.\n\n\\section{Format Independence: Paired Constrained vs.\\ Unconstrained Comparison}\n\\label{app:format-independence}\n\nTo test whether the BCS--accuracy relationship depends on the output format, we run both constrained (enum-based) and unconstrained (free-text) decoding on the same 25 batch type sets from the FewRel 80-relation pool (13 low-BCS, 12 high-BCS; count 8). 551	\section{SCS Embedding Ablation: Name-Only vs. Name+Definition}
552	\label{app:scs-ablation}
553	
554	Table~\ref{tab:scs-ablation} compares predictive accuracy of name-only SCS against name+definition SCS across three datasets.
555	
556	\begin{table}[h]
557	\centering

565	FewRel & 0.178 & 0.193 & +0.015 & 0.848 \\
566	\bottomrule
567	\end{tabular}}

569	\label{tab:scs-ablation}
570	\end{table}
571	
572	Few-NERD is the only dataset whose prompts include type definitions; for MAVEN and FewRel, prompts use type names alone, so name-only SCS directly matches model input. The high matrix correlation (0.66--0.89) confirms that type names capture the dominant semantic structure.
573	
574	\paragraph{Held-out validation.}
575	\label{app:scs-heldout-validation}
576	From the 80-type confusion matrix, we extract the $\binom{16}{2} = 120$ pairs among the 16 validation-origin types (fully disjoint from the 64 training-pool types). The SCS--confusion Spearman correlation remains positive and significant ($\rho = 0.216$, $p = 0.018$), nearly identical to the full 80-type pool ($\rho = 0.218$, $p < 0.001$, $n = 3{,}160$ pairs), ruling out training-set contamination as the driver of the observed relationship.
577	
578	\section{Pair-to-Batch Aggregation Simulation}
579	\label{app:aggregation-sim}
580	
581	The following simulation provides an illustrative explanatory model for how pair-level effects aggregate to batch-level variance. It is not the evidence base for our claims; the observed batch-level $R^2 = 0.218$ (\S\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}) is a direct empirical measurement independent of this simulation.
582	
583	\paragraph{Setup.} We examine whether the pair-level $R^2 \approx 7\%$ (computed over non-zero confusion pairs) can aggregate to the observed batch-level $R^2 \approx 22\%$ via a Monte Carlo simulation. From the FewRel 16-relation experiment, we estimate the inter-pair SCS correlation for pairs sharing a common type ($\rho_\text{SCS} = 0.35$). For batches of $k=8$ types ($\binom{8}{2}=28$ pairs), we simulate 10{,}000 iterations: (1) generate 28 correlated pair-level effects with individual $R^2 = 0.074$; (2) average to obtain batch-level signals; (3) compute batch-level $R^2$.
584	
585	\paragraph{Results.} Simulated batch $R^2 = 0.219$ (95\% CI [0.092, 0.361]), matching the observed value of 0.218. The effective amplification factor is $\sim$3$\times$, explained by signal correlation within batches preserving variance while independent noise averages out (effective degrees of freedom $\approx 3.4$, not 28).
586	
587	\paragraph{Unconditional baseline.}
588	When the simulation uses the unconditional pairwise $r^2 = 0.013$ (computed over all type pairs including zero-confusion pairs; Table~\ref{tab:pairwise}), the simulated batch-level $R^2$ drops to $0.044$ (95\% CI [0.001, 0.151]), well below the observed $R^2 = 0.218$. The analytical formula confirms: $\text{SNR} \times \text{EF} / (\text{SNR} \times \text{EF} + 1) = 0.042$ with the same effective factor $\text{EF} = 3.35$. This ${\sim}5\times$ gap reflects the dilution from zero-confusion pairs: approximately 89\% of type pairs never exhibit mutual confusion, contributing only noise to the unconditional correlation. The conditional model ($R^2 = 0.074 \to 0.219$) matches the observed value because it isolates the minority of semantically proximate pairs that actually compete during type resolution; BCS predicts batch accuracy through the density of \emph{active} confusable pairs, not uniform contributions from all pairs.
589	
590	\paragraph{Sensitivity to $\rho$.}
591	The inter-pair SCS correlation $\rho = 0.35$ is estimated from pairs sharing a common type. At $\rho = 0.20$ (halved correlation), a parallel simulation yields batch $R^2 \approx 0.22$, still matching the observed value; at $\rho = 0.50$, the correlation structure approaches the positive-definiteness boundary; $\rho = 0.35$ thus sits in a stable regime. The simulation is illustrative; the observed batch $R^2 = 0.218$ is the primary evidence.
592	
593	\paragraph{Non-independence and ecological inference.}
594	Types within a batch share prompt context, so their extraction outcomes are not independent---a form of ecological correlation \citep{robinson-1950-ecological}. This non-independence is built into the design: BCS measures batch-level confusability precisely because co-present types interact during type resolution. The core batch-level $R^2 = 0.218\text{--}0.578$ (\S\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}) is a direct empirical observation from regressing batch accuracy on BCS, not derived from the simulation above. The inter-pair SCS correlation $\rho = 0.35$ used in the simulation was estimated from the SCS correlation matrix independently of batch-level accuracy data.
595	
596	\section{BCS Independence from Type Composition}
597	\label{app:bcs-composition}
598	
599	FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per type) makes type frequency invariant across batches, ruling out frequency-driven confounding. To test whether BCS captures information beyond type composition effects, we decompose the explained variance in a joint model (accuracy $\sim$ BCS $+$ mean type difficulty), where type difficulty is the per-type solo-baseline accuracy averaged across batch members. On 100 random 8-type batches from the 16-relation pool, the joint model explains $R^2 = 0.704$. Variance decomposition yields: BCS unique $R^2 = 0.259$ (36.8\% of explained variance), composition unique $R^2 = 0.298$ (42.3\%), and shared $R^2 = 0.147$ (20.9\%). The near-orthogonality of predictors (VIF${}\,=\,1.06$, $r(\text{BCS}, \text{difficulty}) = -0.21$) confirms that BCS captures confusability-specific signal rather than a proxy for type difficulty.
600

629	\section{Split-Origin Covariate Analysis}
630	\label{app:split-origin}
631	
632	FewRel combines 64 train-split and 16 val-split relation types into a single 80-type pool.  Train-origin types show numerically lower accuracy ($-8.8$\,pp, $p = 0.06$; see Limitations), raising the question of whether differential pre-training exposure confounds the BCS--accuracy relationship.  We test this by adding a per-batch \emph{train-origin ratio} (fraction of relation types drawn from the train split) as a covariate in the BCS--accuracy regression.
633	
634	\paragraph{Method.}  Across 213 multi-type batches ($k \geq 2$) from the batch-composition experiment (\S\ref{sec:sdgd}) and the factorial design (\S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}), we compute $\texttt{train\_origin\_ratio} = n_{\text{train}} / k$ for each batch.  The ratio ranges from 0.40 to 1.00 (mean 0.80, consistent with the 64/80 base rate).  We fit:
635	{\small\begin{equation*}
636	\text{acc} = \beta_0 + \beta_{\text{BCS}} \!\cdot\! \text{BCS} + \beta_{\text{orig}} \!\cdot\! \text{train\_origin\_ratio} + \varepsilon
637	\end{equation*}}
638	
639	\paragraph{Results.}  The BCS partial effect is unchanged after controlling for split-origin: $\beta_{\text{BCS}} = -0.416$, $p < 0.001$ (vs.\ $\beta = -0.412$, $p < 0.001$ without the covariate).  Train-origin ratio is not independently significant ($\beta_{\text{origin}} = -0.057$, $p = 0.245$), and $\Delta R^2 = 0.005$.  The BCS--accuracy relationship is therefore not confounded by differential pre-training exposure at the batch level.
640	
641	Pairwise SCS distributions do not differ significantly between train-origin (64 types, mean\,=\,0.880) and val-origin (16 types, mean\,=\,0.874) subsets (Mann-Whitney $p = 0.754$, Cohen's $d = 0.081$); the train/val split does not introduce systematic confusability bias.
642

Seed SD: standard deviation of per-seed means. Ratio = batch variance / seed variance. At count 80, only one batch composition exists, so batch variance vanishes by construction.}
663	\label{tab:variance_decomposition}

665	
666	Table~\ref{tab:variance_decomposition} shows that intra-batch variance dominates inter-seed variance by 5--474$\times$ across count levels 4--64 (mean 125$\times$); random batch composition is the primary source of performance variability. Under greedy decoding (temperature${}\,=\,0$), model inference is nearly deterministic; the inter-seed variance reflects differences in which random batch compositions each seed generates, not inference stochasticity. At count 80, the full schema admits only one composition, leaving residual model-level noise as the sole variance source (seed SD = 0.88\,pp, likely attributable to FP8 quantization non-determinism).
667	
668	\section{Cross-Domain Connections}
669	\label{app:cross-domain}
670	
671	The schema-associated degradation documented here is not unique to information extraction. Schema-Guided Dialogue systems (SGD/SGD-X; \citealt{rastogi2020towards}) face an analogous challenge: as the number of intent and slot types grows, dialogue state tracking accuracy declines. \citet{li2023evaluating} observed count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT-based IE, attributing it to prompt overload. Our two-dimensional decomposition offers a more refined explanation: schema size per se is not the sole predictor; rather, larger schemas increase the probability of including high-SCS pairs, which contribute disproportionately to confusion. This distinction has practical implications: a schema of 80 types with low mutual confusability may outperform a 40-type schema with clustered semantics, and suggests that confusability-aware design principles may generalize to any task where an LLM must discriminate among a prompt-specified label inventory.
672

704	
705	Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_70b} shows that RE degradation slopes are near-identical: $-$30.7\,pp (70B) versus $-$30.1\,pp (32B) from count 8 to count 80, with the 70B model approximately 4\,pp lower in absolute accuracy at each count level. This quantitative match provides strong evidence that Dimension~1 (count-related decline) is directionally consistent across architectures for RE.
706	
707	On NER, the count-related decline direction is consistent across models: both 70B and Qwen3 show a recall decline from count 2 to count 22 (70B: 0.323$\to$0.252, $-$7.1\,pp; Qwen3: 0.515$\to$0.279, $-$23.6\,pp). Both models also exhibit a U-shaped rebound at higher counts (70B: 0.252 at count 22 $\to$ 0.297 at count 44; Qwen3: 0.279 at count 22 $\to$ 0.407 at count 66), which cross-validates the U-shape phenomenon. The magnitude difference reflects a precision-recall tradeoff: 70B operates more conservatively (higher precision, lower recall), while Qwen3 over-predicts (higher recall, lower precision, especially at low counts).
708	
709	For the confusability dimension (Dimension 2), at count 16 on FewRel, LLaMA-70B achieves low-BCS accuracy of 0.708 versus high-BCS accuracy of 0.599 ($\Delta = +10.9$\,pp, 95\% CI [$+$3.0, $+$20.1]). The confidence interval excludes zero; the confusability effect is thus confirmed at the 70B scale.
710	
711	In summary, FewRel RE provides a quantitative match across models (near-identical slopes for Dimension~1 and a significant Dimension~2 effect). NER provides directional consistency: the count-related decline direction is preserved and the U-shaped rebound replicates, though magnitudes vary with each model's precision-recall operating point. Together, these results indicate that the degradation pattern is directionally consistent across architectures.
712	
713	\section{Cross-Model Validation with Gemini 2.5 Flash (API)}
714	\label{sec:appendix_cross_model_gemini}
715	
716	We validate both dimensions on a proprietary model, Gemini 2.5 Flash \citep{gemini-2025}, accessed via API. This extends the cross-model evidence from open-weight families (Qwen, LLaMA) to a proprietary architecture with unknown training details. We replicate the FewRel count-curve and BCS-controlled experiments using the same 960 balanced samples, batch compositions, and evaluation protocol as Qwen3-32B.
717	
718	\begin{table}[h]
719	\centering
720	\small
721	\begin{tabular}{lrr}
722	\toprule
723	\textbf{Count} & \textbf{Gemini Flash} & \textbf{Qwen3-32B} \\
724	\midrule
725	1  & 0.827 & --- \\
726	4  & 0.873 & 0.870 \\
727	8  & 0.820 & 0.805 \\
728	16 & 0.787 & 0.719 \\
729	32 & 0.739 & 0.652 \\
730	64$^\dagger$ & 0.637 & 0.539 \\
731	\bottomrule
732	\end{tabular}
733	\caption{Cross-model count curves for Gemini 2.5 Flash (API) and Qwen3-32B on FewRel RE (classification accuracy). 25 batches per count level, same samples and batch compositions (seed 42). Gemini's count-1 result is included for completeness; Qwen3 count-1 was not collected. Both models show monotonic decline from count 4 onward ($\rho = -1.0$ for both on counts 4--32). $^\dagger$Count 64: Gemini based on 11/25 batches (partial).}
734	\label{tab:cross_model_gemini}
735	\end{table}
736	
737	Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_gemini} confirms that the count-related accuracy decline replicates on Gemini 2.5 Flash. From count 4 to count 32, both models show perfectly monotonic decline (Spearman $\rho = -1.0$). Including count 1, the Gemini count curve yields $\rho = -0.900$ ($p = 0.037$); including partial count-64 data (11/25 batches), $\rho = -0.943$ ($p = 0.005$). The decline magnitude is smaller for Gemini ($-$13.3\,pp from count 4 to 32) than for Qwen3 ($-$21.8\,pp), consistent with Gemini's higher absolute accuracy providing more headroom before degradation saturates. At each count level, Gemini matches or exceeds Qwen3, with the gap widening at higher counts (0.3\,pp at count 4 vs.\ 8.7\,pp at count 32, 9.8\,pp at partial count 64); Gemini appears more robust to schema scaling but not immune.
738	
739	\paragraph{Dimension~2: BCS-controlled confusability.}
740	We replicate the BCS-controlled experiment on Gemini 2.5 Flash using extreme-BCS batches at count 8 ($n = 50$; 25 low-BCS, 25 high-BCS) and count 16 ($n = 10$; 5+5).
741	
742	At count 8, low-BCS batches achieve 90.5\% vs.\ high-BCS 72.9\% ($\Delta = -17.6$\,pp, 95\% CI [15.1, 20.1]). At count 16, the gap narrows to $-$8.7\,pp, but one high-BCS outlier (BCS${}=0.97$, accuracy 59.9\%) inflates Pearson $r$; leave-one-out excluding this point drops $r$ from $-$0.80 to $-$0.42 ($p = 0.22$) and $\rho$ from $-$0.40 to $-$0.18 ($p = 0.64$), confirming the count-16 correlation is driven by this single leverage point. The count-8 gap is smaller than Qwen3-32B's ($-$31.2\,pp), consistent with ceiling compression: Gemini's higher baseline (90.5\% vs.\ 82.3\%) leaves less room for degradation.
743	
744	Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs_summary} summarizes BCS-controlled results across all validated models. Both dimensions thus replicate on a proprietary architecture with unknown training details.

764	\section{Few-Shot Prompting and Confusability}
765	\label{sec:appendix_fewshot_bcs}
766	
767	To test whether in-context demonstrations mitigate the BCS-associated confusability effect, we evaluate 0-shot, 1-shot, and 5-shot prompting on FewRel 80-relation RE at count 8 using the same extreme-BCS batch design as \S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}. Each condition uses 50 batches (25 low-BCS, mean $\approx 0.36$; 25 high-BCS, mean $\approx 0.80$), drawn from the pre-constructed batch pool of \S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}. Few-shot examples are sampled from the FewRel training split for each target type (Table~\ref{tab:fewshot_bcs}).
768	
769	\begin{table}[h]
770	\centering
771	\small
772	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{\begin{tabular}{lrrrl}
773	\toprule
774	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Low-BCS} & \textbf{High-BCS} & \textbf{$\Delta$ (pp)} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
775	\midrule
776	0-shot & 0.682 & 0.498 & +18.5 & {[}+14.3, +22.7{]} \\
777	1-shot & 0.754 & 0.554 & +20.0 & {[}+16.3, +23.5{]} \\
778	5-shot & 0.715 & 0.488 & +22.8 & {[}+18.9, +26.8{]} \\
779	\bottomrule
780	\end{tabular}}
781	\caption{BCS gap under few-shot prompting (FewRel, count 8). Demonstrations improve absolute accuracy on low-BCS batches (+3.3--7.2\,pp over 0-shot) but provide inconsistent benefit on high-BCS batches (+5.6\,pp at 1-shot, $-$1.0\,pp at 5-shot). The confusability gap widens from +18.5\,pp (0-shot) to +22.8\,pp (5-shot). All metrics are micro-averaged across batches.}
782	\label{tab:fewshot_bcs}
783	\end{table}
784	
785	Demonstrations improve absolute accuracy primarily on low-BCS (easy) batches, where type boundaries are already distinct and examples reinforce correct mappings. On high-BCS batches, where types are semantically confusable, demonstrations provide inconsistent benefit (+5.6\,pp at 1-shot, $-$1.0\,pp at 5-shot): the model still substitutes similar types at comparable rates. The net effect is that the confusability gap widens under few-shot prompting (+4.3\,pp from 0-shot to 5-shot, bootstrap $p = 0.014$), with all three confidence intervals excluding zero. This indicates that BCS-associated type confusion is a structural property of the schema composition rather than a deficit addressable through prompt engineering. The non-monotonic low-BCS pattern (0.754 at 1-shot vs.\ 0.715 at 5-shot) may reflect lost-in-middle effects from increased prompt length \citep{lost-in-middle-2024}.
786	
787	The 0-shot gap (+18.5\,pp) is smaller than the extreme-batch gap reported in \S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled} (+25.5\,pp) because the two experiments use different test splits and batch sampling seeds; direct magnitude comparison across experiments is not appropriate.
788
