858	\section{Schema Label Renaming Experiment}
859	\label{app:renaming}
860	
861	To test whether reducing pairwise SCS through label renaming improves accuracy, we conducted a controlled intervention on FewRel 80-relation RE (count 8, Qwen3-32B). We identified 14 high-SCS relation pairs (covering 12 unique relation types, as two types each appear in multiple pairs; mean SCS = 0.87) and replaced one label in each pair with a semantically distinct paraphrase chosen to minimize embedding cosine similarity (all-MiniLM-L6-v2). Examples: \textit{participant of} $\to$ \textit{event involvement} (SCS: 0.97 $\to$ 0.72), \textit{follows} $\to$ \textit{prior entry} (0.90 $\to$ 0.69), \textit{part of} $\to$ \textit{integral segment of} (0.90 $\to$ 0.64). The renaming reduced mean pairwise SCS from 0.87 to 0.67 ($\Delta = -0.20$). We ran the same 50 BCS-controlled batches (25 low-BCS, 25 high-BCS; 96 samples each) under both original (control) and renamed (treatment) label sets (Table~\ref{tab:renaming}).
862	
863	\begin{table}[h!]
864	\centering
865	\small
866	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
867	\toprule
868	& \textbf{Control} & \textbf{Treatment} & \textbf{$\Delta$\,(pp)} \\
869	\midrule
870	Low-BCS ($n=25$)  & 90.1 & 87.1 & $-$3.0 \\
871	High-BCS ($n=25$) & 69.3 & 60.5 & $-$8.8 \\
872	\bottomrule
873	\end{tabular}
874	\caption{Accuracy (\%) under original vs.\ renamed labels on FewRel count-8 batches. Renaming reduced embedding similarity but decreased accuracy in both groups, with a larger drop on high-BCS batches (Cohen's $d = -2.92$) than low-BCS ($d = -1.09$). Interaction: $\Delta_{\text{high}} - \Delta_{\text{low}} = -5.9$\,pp ($t = -7.23$, $p < 0.001$).}
875	\label{tab:renaming}
876	\end{table}
877	
878	Renaming hurt accuracy uniformly: both groups showed significant declines (paired $t$-test, both $p < 0.001$). The number of renamed types per batch correlated negatively with accuracy change ($r = -0.625$, $p < 0.001$). A solo sanity check confirmed that 11 of 12 renamed labels were individually recognizable (accuracy $\geq$ original $-5$\,pp). The results indicate that replacing familiar Wikidata labels with unfamiliar paraphrases disrupts the model's type semantics; this label unfamiliarity effect outweighs any benefit from reduced confusability.
879	
880	\section{Relaxed Matching Evaluation (NER)}
881	\label{app:relaxed-matching}
882	
883	To test whether exact-match F1 masks the BCS signal in NER by conflating boundary errors with type errors, we evaluate three progressively relaxed matching criteria on the full Few-NERD NER dataset ($n = 450$ batches: 6 count levels $\times$ 25 batches $\times$ 3 seeds; Table~\ref{tab:relaxed_matching}).
884	
885	\begin{table}[h!]
886	\centering
887	\small
888	\begin{tabular}{lrrrrr}
889	\toprule
890	\textbf{Match mode} & \textbf{$r$} & \textbf{$p$} & \textbf{$\rho$} & \textbf{$p$} & \textbf{F1} \\
891	\midrule
892	Exact        & 0.014 & 0.760 & $-$0.101 & 0.033 & 0.304 \\
893	Relaxed span & 0.003 & 0.946 & $-$0.110 & 0.020 & 0.346 \\
894	Token overlap & 0.004 & 0.936 & $-$0.113 & 0.017 & 0.349 \\
895	Type-ignored & 0.023 & 0.621 & $-$0.097 & 0.039 & 0.425 \\
896	\bottomrule
897	\end{tabular}
898	\caption{BCS--F1 correlation under progressively relaxed NER matching. Relaxed span credits a match if the predicted span overlaps the gold span and the type is correct; token overlap uses token-level intersection; type-ignored drops the type constraint entirely. Relaxed matching improves mean F1 by 4.2\,pp (98.4\% of batches improve), but Pearson $r$ remains near zero across all modes (bootstrap 95\% CI crosses zero in all cases). Boundary errors affect all batches uniformly and do not mask a BCS signal.}
899	\label{tab:relaxed_matching}
900	\end{table}
901	
902	A per-error-mode analysis confirms this null extends to all individual categories: miss ($\rho = 0.017$), boundary ($\rho = -0.043$), and type substitution ($\rho = -0.010$) rates show no BCS association (all $p > 0.3$, $n = 450$). The null holds within each count level (count 2: $r = 0.022$; count 6: $r = 0.110$; count 12: $r = 0.028$; all $p > 0.3$), ruling out count as a confound.

This does not invalidate the confusability effect; the within-pool slope is significant ($p=0.027$) when BCS is deliberately varied, but establishes that natural schema sampling at this scale does not produce sufficient BCS contrast for detection.\n\n\\section{SCS Embedding Ablation: Name-Only vs. Name+Definition}\n\\label{app:scs-ablation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scs-ablation} compares predictive accuracy of name-only SCS against name+definition SCS across three datasets.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nDataset & SCS$_\\text{name}$ $r$ & SCS$_\\text{def}$ $r$ & $\\Delta r$ & Matrix corr. \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & 0.172 & 0.149 & $-$0.023 & 0.894 \\\\\nMAVEN & 0.129 & 0.164 & +0.035 & 0.656 \\\\\nFewRel & 0.178 & 0.193 & +0.015 & 0.848 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Pearson correlation between SCS and empirical confusion rate. ``Def'' concatenates type name with definition before embedding. All correlations significant ($p < 0.001$). The inconsistent direction of $\\Delta r$ indicates that definitions provide no systematic benefit.}\n\\label{tab:scs-ablation}\n\\end{table}\n\nFew-NERD is the only dataset whose prompts include type definitions; for MAVEN and FewRel, prompts use type names alone, so name-only SCS directly matches model input. The high matrix correlation (0.66--0.89) confirms that type names capture the dominant semantic structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Held-out validation.}\n\\label{app:scs-heldout-validation}\nFrom the 80-type confusion matrix, we extract the $\\binom{16}{2} = 120$ pairs among the 16 validation-origin types (fully disjoint from the 64 training-pool types). The SCS--confusion Spearman correlation remains positive and significant ($\\rho = 0.216$, $p = 0.018$), nearly identical to the full 80-type pool ($\\rho = 0.218$, $p < 0.001$, $n = 3{,}160$ pairs), ruling out training-set contamination as the driver of the observed relationship.\n\n\\section{Pair-to-Batch Aggregation Simulation}\n\\label{app:aggregation-sim}\n\nThe following simulation provides an illustrative explanatory model for how pair-level effects aggregate to batch-level variance. It is not the evidence base for our claims; the observed batch-level $R^2 = 0.218$ (\\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}) is a direct empirical measurement independent of this simulation.\n\n\\paragraph{Setup.} We examine whether the pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ (computed over non-zero confusion pairs) can aggregate to the observed batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ via a Monte Carlo simulation. From the FewRel 16-relation experiment, we estimate the inter-pair SCS correlation for pairs sharing a common type ($\\rho_\\text{SCS} = 0.35$). For batches of $k=8$ types ($\\binom{8}{2}=28$ pairs), we simulate 10{,}000 iterations: (1) generate 28 correlated pair-level effects with individual $R^2 = 0.074$; (2) average to obtain batch-level signals; (3) compute batch-level $R^2$.\n\n\\paragraph{Results.} Simulated batch $R^2 = 0.219$ (95\\% CI [0.092, 0.361]), matching the observed value of 0.218. The effective amplification factor is $\\sim$3$\\times$, explained by signal correlation within batches preserving variance while independent noise averages out (effective degrees of freedom $\\approx 3.4$, not 28).\n\n\\paragraph{Unconditional baseline.}\nWhen the simulation uses the unconditional pairwise $r^2 = 0.013$ (computed over all type pairs including zero-confusion pairs; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}), the simulated batch-level $R^2$ drops to $0.044$ (95\\% CI [0.001, 0.151]), well below the observed $R^2 = 0.218$. The analytical formula confirms: $\\text{SNR} \\times \\text{EF} / (\\text{SNR} \\times \\text{EF} + 1) = 0.042$ with the same effective factor $\\text{EF} = 3.35$. This ${\\sim}5\\times$ gap reflects the dilution from zero-confusion pairs: approximately 89\\% of type pairs never exhibit mutual confusion, contributing only noise to the unconditional correlation. The conditional model ($R^2 = 0.074 \\to 0.219$) matches the observed value because it isolates the minority of semantically proximate pairs that actually compete during type resolution; BCS predicts batch accuracy through the density of \\emph{active} confusable pairs, not uniform contributions from all pairs.\n\n\\paragraph{Sensitivity to $\\rho$.}\nThe inter-pair SCS correlation $\\rho = 0.35$ is estimated from pairs sharing a common type. At $\\rho = 0.20$ (halved correlation), a parallel simulation yields batch $R^2 \\approx 0.22$, still matching the observed value; at $\\rho = 0.50$, the correlation structure approaches the positive-definiteness boundary; $\\rho = 0.35$ thus sits in a stable regime. The simulation is illustrative; the observed batch $R^2 = 0.218$ is the primary evidence.\n\n\\paragraph{Non-independence and ecological inference.}\nTypes within a batch share prompt context, so their extraction outcomes are not independent---a form of ecological correlation \\citep{robinson-1950-ecological}. This non-independence is built into the design: BCS measures batch-level confusability precisely because co-present types interact during type resolution. The core batch-level $R^2 = 0.218\\text{--}0.578$ (\\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}) is a direct empirical observation from regressing batch accuracy on BCS, not derived from the simulation above. The inter-pair SCS correlation $\\rho = 0.35$ used in the simulation was estimated from the SCS correlation matrix independently of batch-level accuracy data.\n\n\\section{BCS Independence from Type Composition}\n\\label{app:bcs-composition}\n\nFewRel's balanced design (700 instances per type) makes type frequency invariant across batches, ruling out frequency-driven confounding. To test whether BCS captures information beyond type composition effects, we decompose the explained variance in a joint model (accuracy $\\sim$ BCS $+$ mean type difficulty), where type difficulty is the per-type solo-baseline accuracy averaged across batch members. On 100 random 8-type batches from the 16-relation pool, the joint model explains $R^2 = 0.704$. Variance decomposition yields: BCS unique $R^2 = 0.259$ (36.8\\% of explained variance), composition unique $R^2 = 0.298$ (42.3\\%), and shared $R^2 = 0.147$ (20.9\\%). The near-orthogonality of predictors (VIF${}\\,=\\,1.06$, $r(\\text{BCS}, \\text{difficulty}) = -0.21$) confirms that BCS captures confusability-specific signal rather than a proxy for type difficulty.\n\n\\section{SCS vs.\\ Simple Baselines}\n\\label{app:scs-baselines}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:scs_baselines} compares SCS against simple baselines for pairwise confusion prediction.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Metric} & \\textbf{Spearman $\\rho$} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFewRel & SCS (embedding) & \\textbf{0.218} & $< 0.001$ \\\\\n(3{,}160 pairs) & Same-parent (WordNet) & 0.097 & $< 0.001$ \\\\\n& WordNet Wu-Palmer & 0.073 & $< 0.001$ \\\\\n& Levenshtein & 0.016 & 0.363 (n.s.) \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & SCS (embedding) & \\textbf{0.166} & $< 0.001$ \\\\\n(1{,}953 pairs) & Levenshtein & 0.152 & $< 0.001$ \\\\\n& Same-parent (prefix) & 0.146 & $< 0.001$ \\\\\n& WordNet Wu-Palmer & 0.054 & 0.070 (n.s.)$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Pairwise confusion prediction: SCS vs.\\ simple baselines. Spearman $\\rho$ between each metric and the empirical confusion rate. SCS outperforms all baselines on FewRel; the margin is smaller on Few-NERD, where hierarchical naming conventions provide partial signal to string-based metrics. $^\\dagger$42\\% of pairs lack WordNet coverage.}\n\\label{tab:scs_baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Split-Origin Covariate Analysis}\n\\label{app:split-origin}\n\nFewRel combines 64 train-split and 16 val-split relation types into a single 80-type pool.  Train-origin types show numerically lower accuracy ($-8.8$\\,pp, $p = 0.06$; see Limitations), raising the question of whether differential pre-training exposure confounds the BCS--accuracy relationship.  We test this by adding a per-batch \\emph{train-origin ratio} (fraction of relation types drawn from the train split) as a covariate in the BCS--accuracy regression.\n\n\\paragraph{Method.}  Across 213 multi-type batches ($k \\geq 2$) from the batch-composition experiment (\\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}) and the factorial design (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}), we compute $\\texttt{train\\_origin\\_ratio} = n_{\\text{train}} / k$ for each batch.  The ratio ranges from 0.40 to 1.00 (mean 0.80, consistent with the 64/80 base rate).  We fit:\n{\\small\\begin{equation*}\n\\text{acc} = \\beta_0 + \\beta_{\\text{BCS}} \\!\\cdot\\! \\text{BCS} + \\beta_{\\text{orig}} \\!\\cdot\\! \\text{train\\_origin\\_ratio} + \\varepsilon\n\\end{equation*}}\n\n\\paragraph{Results.}  The BCS partial effect is unchanged after controlling for split-origin: $\\beta_{\\text{BCS}} = -0.416$, $p < 0.001$ (vs.\\ $\\beta = -0.412$, $p < 0.001$ without the covariate).  Train-origin ratio is not independently significant ($\\beta_{\\text{origin}} = -0.057$, $p = 0.245$), and $\\Delta R^2 = 0.005$.  The BCS--accuracy relationship is therefore not confounded by differential pre-training exposure at the batch level.\n\nPairwise SCS distributions do not differ significantly between train-origin (64 types, mean\\,=\\,0.880) and val-origin (16 types, mean\\,=\\,0.874) subsets (Mann-Whitney $p = 0.754$, Cohen's $d = 0.081$); the train/val split does not introduce systematic confusability bias.\n\n\\section{Cross-Seed Variance Decomposition}\n\\label{app:variance-decomposition}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{rrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Batch SD} & \\textbf{Seed SD} & \\textbf{Ratio} \\\\\n\\midrule\n4 & 0.0889 & 0.0122 & 53.5 \\\\\n8 & 0.0820 & 0.0098 & 70.0 \\\\\n16 & 0.0626 & 0.0270 & 5.4 \\\\\n32 & 0.0462 & 0.0091 & 26.0 \\\\\n40 & 0.0421 & 0.0019 & 474.4 \\\\\n64 & 0.0245 & 0.0022 & 119.8 \\\\\n80 & 0.0001 & 0.0088 & $<$0.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition on FewRel (3 seeds $\\times$ 25 batches per count level). Batch SD: root-mean intra-seed variance averaged over seeds. Seed SD: standard deviation of per-seed means. Ratio = batch variance / seed variance. At count 80, only one batch composition exists, so batch variance vanishes by construction.}\n\\label{tab:variance_decomposition}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:variance_decomposition} shows that intra-batch variance dominates inter-seed variance by 5--474$\\times$ across count levels 4--64 (mean 125$\\times$); random batch composition is the primary source of performance variability. Under greedy decoding (temperature${}\\,=\\,0$), model inference is nearly deterministic; the inter-seed variance reflects differences in which random batch compositions each seed generates, not inference stochasticity. At count 80, the full schema admits only one composition, leaving residual model-level noise as the sole variance source (seed SD = 0.88\\,pp, likely attributable to FP8 quantization non-determinism).\n\n\\section{Cross-Domain Connections}\n\\label{app:cross-domain}\n\nThe schema-associated degradation documented here is not unique to information extraction. Schema-Guided Dialogue systems (SGD/SGD-X; \\citealt{rastogi2020towards}) face an analogous challenge: as the number of intent and slot types grows, dialogue state tracking accuracy declines. \\citet{li2023evaluating} observed count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT-based IE, attributing it to prompt overload. Our two-dimensional decomposition offers a more refined explanation: schema size per se is not the sole predictor; rather, larger schemas increase the probability of including high-SCS pairs, which contribute disproportionately to confusion. This distinction has practical implications: a schema of 80 types with low mutual confusability may outperform a 40-type schema with clustered semantics, and suggests that confusability-aware design principles may generalize to any task where an LLM must discriminate among a prompt-specified label inventory.\n\n\\section{Cross-Model Validation with LLaMA-3.1-70B-Instruct (FP8)}\n\\label{sec:appendix_cross_model_70b}\n\nTo test whether the two-dimensional degradation pattern generalizes across models, we replicate the count-curve and BCS-controlled experiments on Meta-LLaMA-3.1-70B-Instruct (FP8 quantization). All batch compositions, type pools, and test samples are identical to those used for Qwen3-32B (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}, \\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{LLaMA-70B} & \\textbf{Qwen3-32B} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{RE (FewRel, Classification Accuracy)}} \\\\\n8  & 0.757 & 0.805 \\\\\n16 & 0.685 & 0.719 \\\\\n32 & 0.601 & 0.652 \\\\\n64 & 0.504 & 0.539 \\\\\n80 & 0.450 & 0.504 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{NER (Few-NERD, Text+Type Match Recall)}} \\\\\n2  & 0.323 & 0.515 \\\\\n6  & 0.300 & 0.399 \\\\\n12 & 0.278 & 0.318 \\\\\n22 & 0.252 & 0.279 \\\\\n44 & 0.297 & 0.309 \\\\\n66 & 0.273 & 0.407 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cross-model count curves for LLaMA-3.1-70B-Instruct (FP8) and Qwen3-32B. RE decline (count 8$\\to$80): $-$30.7\\,pp (70B) vs.\\ $-$30.1\\,pp (32B). NER decline (count 2$\\to$22): $-$7.1\\,pp (70B) vs.\\ $-$23.6\\,pp (32B); both models exhibit a U-shaped rebound at higher counts. 25 batches per count level, same samples and batch compositions across models (seed 42). All metrics are micro-averaged across batches.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model_70b}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model_70b} shows that RE degradation slopes are near-identical: $-$30.7\\,pp (70B) versus $-$30.1\\,pp (32B) from count 8 to count 80, with the 70B model approximately 4\\,pp lower in absolute accuracy at each count level. This quantitative match provides strong evidence that Dimension~1 (count-related decline) is directionally consistent across architectures for RE.\n\nOn NER, the count-related decline direction is consistent across models: both 70B and Qwen3 show a recall decline from count 2 to count 22 (70B: 0.323$\\to$0.252, $-$7.1\\,pp; Qwen3: 0.515$\\to$0.279, $-$23.6\\,pp). Both models also exhibit a U-shaped rebound at higher counts (70B: 0.252 at count 22 $\\to$ 0.297 at count 44; Qwen3: 0.279 at count 22 $\\to$ 0.407 at count 66), which cross-validates the U-shape phenomenon. The magnitude difference reflects a precision-recall tradeoff: 70B operates more conservatively (higher precision, lower recall), while Qwen3 over-predicts (higher recall, lower precision, especially at low counts).\n\nFor the confusability dimension (Dimension 2), at count 16 on FewRel, LLaMA-70B achieves low-BCS accuracy of 0.708 versus high-BCS accuracy of 0.599 ($\\Delta = +10.9$\\,pp, 95\\% CI [$+$3.0, $+$20.1]). The confidence interval excludes zero; the confusability effect is thus confirmed at the 70B scale.\n\nIn summary, FewRel RE provides a quantitative match across models (near-identical slopes for Dimension~1 and a significant Dimension~2 effect). NER provides directional consistency: the count-related decline direction is preserved and the U-shaped rebound replicates, though magnitudes vary with each model's precision-recall operating point. Together, these results indicate that the degradation pattern is directionally consistent across architectures.\n\n\\section{Cross-Model Validation with Gemini 2.5 Flash (API)}\n\\label{sec:appendix_cross_model_gemini}\n\nWe validate both dimensions on a proprietary model, Gemini 2.5 Flash \\citep{gemini-2025}, accessed via API. This extends the cross-model evidence from open-weight families (Qwen, LLaMA) to a proprietary architecture with unknown training details. We replicate the FewRel count-curve and BCS-controlled experiments using the same 960 balanced samples, batch compositions, and evaluation protocol as Qwen3-32B.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Gemini Flash} & \\textbf{Qwen3-32B} \\\\\n\\midrule\n1  & 0.827 & --- \\\\\n4  & 0.873 & 0.870 \\\\\n8  & 0.820 & 0.805 \\\\\n16 & 0.787 & 0.719 \\\\\n32 & 0.739 & 0.652 \\\\\n64$^\\dagger$ & 0.637 & 0.539 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cross-model count curves for Gemini 2.5 Flash (API) and Qwen3-32B on FewRel RE (classification accuracy). 25 batches per count level, same samples and batch compositions (seed 42). Gemini's count-1 result is included for completeness; Qwen3 count-1 was not collected. Both models show monotonic decline from count 4 onward ($\\rho = -1.0$ for both on counts 4--32). $^\\dagger$Count 64: Gemini based on 11/25 batches (partial).}\n\\label{tab:cross_model_gemini}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model_gemini} confirms that the count-related accuracy decline replicates on Gemini 2.5 Flash. From count 4 to count 32, both models show perfectly monotonic decline (Spearman $\\rho = -1.0$). Including count 1, the Gemini count curve yields $\\rho = -0.900$ ($p = 0.037$); including partial count-64 data (11/25 batches), $\\rho = -0.943$ ($p = 0.005$). The decline magnitude is smaller for Gemini ($-$13.3\\,pp from count 4 to 32) than for Qwen3 ($-$21.8\\,pp), consistent with Gemini's higher absolute accuracy providing more headroom before degradation saturates. At each count level, Gemini matches or exceeds Qwen3, with the gap widening at higher counts (0.3\\,pp at count 4 vs.\\ 8.7\\,pp at count 32, 9.8\\,pp at partial count 64); Gemini appears more robust to schema scaling but not immune.\n\n\\paragraph{Dimension~2: BCS-controlled confusability.}\nWe replicate the BCS-controlled experiment on Gemini 2.5 Flash using extreme-BCS batches at count 8 ($n = 50$; 25 low-BCS, 25 high-BCS) and count 16 ($n = 10$; 5+5).\n\nAt count 8, low-BCS batches achieve 90.5\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 72.9\\% ($\\Delta = -17.6$\\,pp, 95\\% CI [15.1, 20.1]). At count 16, the gap narrows to $-$8.7\\,pp, but one high-BCS outlier (BCS${}=0.97$, accuracy 59.9\\%) inflates Pearson $r$; leave-one-out excluding this point drops $r$ from $-$0.80 to $-$0.42 ($p = 0.22$) and $\\rho$ from $-$0.40 to $-$0.18 ($p = 0.64$), confirming the count-16 correlation is driven by this single leverage point. The count-8 gap is smaller than Qwen3-32B's ($-$31.2\\,pp), consistent with ceiling compression: Gemini's higher baseline (90.5\\% vs.\\ 82.3\\%) leaves less room for degradation.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs_summary} summarizes BCS-controlled results across all validated models. Both dimensions thus replicate on a proprietary architecture with unknown training details.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Count} & \\textbf{Low BCS} & \\textbf{High BCS} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-32B     & 8  & 82.3\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$31.2\\,pp & --- \\\\\nQwen3-32B     & 16 & 82.8\\% & 51.6\\% & $-$31.2\\,pp & --- \\\\\nLlama-8B      & 8  & 78.1\\% & 51.0\\% & $-$27.1\\,pp & --- \\\\\nLlama-70B FP8 & 16 & ---    & ---    & $-$10.9\\,pp & {[}3.0, 20.1{]} \\\\\nGemini Flash  & 8  & 90.5\\% & 72.9\\% & $-$17.6\\,pp & {[}15.1, 20.1{]} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Cross-model BCS-controlled FewRel RE summary. All models show significant low-vs-high BCS accuracy gaps. Gap magnitude varies with model capacity (ceiling compression), but the direction is consistent across open-weight (8B--70B) and proprietary architectures.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model_bcs_summary}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Few-Shot Prompting and Confusability}\n\\label{sec:appendix_fewshot_bcs}\n\nTo test whether in-context demonstrations mitigate the BCS-associated confusability effect, we evaluate 0-shot, 1-shot, and 5-shot prompting on FewRel 80-relation RE at count 8 using the same extreme-BCS batch design as \\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}. Each condition uses 50 batches (25 low-BCS, mean $\\approx 0.36$; 25 high-BCS, mean $\\approx 0.80$), drawn from the pre-constructed batch pool of \\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}. Few-shot examples are sampled from the FewRel training split for each target type (Table~\\ref{tab:fewshot_bcs}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\\begin{tabular}{lrrrl}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Low-BCS} & \\textbf{High-BCS} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\n0-shot & 0.682 & 0.498 & +18.5 & {[}+14.3, +22.7{]} \\\\\n1-shot & 0.754 & 0.554 & +20.0 & {[}+16.3, +23.5{]} \\\\\n5-shot & 0.715 & 0.488 & +22.8 & {[}+18.9, +26.8{]} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{BCS gap under few-shot prompting (FewRel, count 8). Demonstrations improve absolute accuracy on low-BCS batches (+3.3--7.2\\,pp over 0-shot) but provide inconsistent benefit on high-BCS batches (+5.6\\,pp at 1-shot, $-$1.0\\,pp at 5-shot). The confusability gap widens from +18.5\\,pp (0-shot) to +22.8\\,pp (5-shot). All metrics are micro-averaged across batches.}\n\\label{tab:fewshot_bcs}\n\\end{table}\n\nDemonstrations improve absolute accuracy primarily on low-BCS (easy) batches, where type boundaries are already distinct and examples reinforce correct mappings. On high-BCS batches, where types are semantically confusable, demonstrations provide inconsistent benefit (+5.6\\,pp at 1-shot, $-$1.0\\,pp at 5-shot): the model still substitutes similar types at comparable rates. The net effect is that the confusability gap widens under few-shot prompting (+4.3\\,pp from 0-shot to 5-shot, bootstrap $p = 0.014$), with all three confidence intervals excluding zero. This indicates that BCS-associated type confusion is a structural property of the schema composition rather than a deficit addressable through prompt engineering. The non-monotonic low-BCS pattern (0.754 at 1-shot vs.\\ 0.715 at 5-shot) may reflect lost-in-middle effects from increased prompt length \\citep{lost-in-middle-2024}.\n\nThe 0-shot gap (+18.5\\,pp) is smaller than the extreme-batch gap reported in \\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled} (+25.5\\,pp) because the two experiments use different test splits and batch sampling seeds; direct magnitude comparison across experiments is not appropriate.\n\n\\section{Qualitative Error Analysis}\n\\label{app:error-analysis}\n\nTables~\\ref{tab:top10_re} and~\\ref{tab:top10_ner} list the ten most frequently confused type pairs on FewRel 80-relation RE and Few-NERD NER. Confusion counts are aggregated bidirectionally across three random seeds (72{,}000 predictions per seed for RE; single-seed 66-type extraction for NER). Table~\\ref{tab:error_examples} provides illustrative confusion instances with SCS values.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{clllr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{\\#} & \\textbf{Type A} & \\textbf{Type B} & \\textbf{SCS} & \\textbf{$N$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & instance of & taxon rank & 0.53 & 875 \\\\\n2 & child & father & 0.84 & 700 \\\\\n3 & participant of & participating team & 0.75 & 675 \\\\\n4 & mountain range & part of & 0.57 & 575 \\\\\n5 & mouth of watercourse & tributary & 0.65 & 549 \\\\\n6 & child & mother & 0.84 & 500 \\\\\n7 & league & member of & 0.66 & 450 \\\\\n8 & country of citizenship & country of origin & 0.76 & 400 \\\\\n9 & followed by & follows & 0.90 & 400 \\\\\n10 & occupation & voice type & 0.57 & 400 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Top-10 confused relation pairs on FewRel 80-relation RE. $N$: bidirectional confusion count aggregated over 3 seeds $\\times$ 25 batches. SCS: pairwise Schema Confusability Score (Qwen3 embedding cosine similarity). High-SCS pairs (e.g., \\textit{followed by}/\\textit{follows}, SCS\\,=\\,0.90) and family-relation pairs (\\textit{child}/\\textit{father}, \\textit{child}/\\textit{mother}) dominate. Some low-SCS pairs (\\textit{instance of}/\\textit{taxon rank}, SCS\\,=\\,0.53) reflect domain-specific co-occurrence rather than semantic similarity.}\n\\label{tab:top10_re}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{clllr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{\\#} & \\textbf{Type A} & \\textbf{Type B} & \\textbf{SCS} & \\textbf{$N$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & person-other & person-politician & 0.93 & 121 \\\\\n2 & location-GPE & organization-other & 0.83 & 81 \\\\\n3 & art-film & location-GPE & 0.69 & 57 \\\\\n4 & building-other & location-GPE & 0.85 & 56 \\\\\n5 & building-other & person-other & 0.90 & 43 \\\\\n6 & location-GPE & product-other & 0.69 & 39 \\\\\n7 & org-gov/agency & person-other & 0.85 & 34 \\\\\n8 & person-actor & person-other & 0.90 & 34 \\\\\n9 & building-other & location-road/transit & 0.76 & 32 \\\\\n10 & location-GPE & org-sportsleague & 0.81 & 30 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Top-10 confused entity type pairs on Few-NERD NER. $N$: bidirectional confusion count from 66-type full-schema extraction. SCS: pairwise Schema Confusability Score (Qwen3 embedding cosine similarity). Intra-category confusions (e.g., \\textit{person-other}/\\textit{person-politician}, SCS\\,=\\,0.93) and cross-category pairs sharing referent overlap (\\textit{building-other}/\\textit{location-GPE}, SCS\\,=\\,0.85) are most frequent. The ``other'' subtypes absorb diverse entities and confuse with more specific siblings.}\n\\label{tab:top10_ner}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{p{8cm}llc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Sentence} & \\textbf{Gold} & \\textbf{Pred.} & \\textbf{SCS} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\ldots\\ played for \\textbf{Valencia} \\ldots\\ [\\textbf{EuroLeague}] & participant & participant of & .97 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\textbf{Pilões} is a municipality in \\textbf{Rio Grande do Norte} \\ldots & contains a.t.e. & located in a.t.e. & .92 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nDavies \\ldots\\ appointed to \\textbf{Cabinet} as \\textbf{Sec.\\ of State} & part of & member of & .90 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Illustrative confusion instances from FewRel 80-rel RE. All gold-predicted pairs have SCS\\,$>$\\,0.90: \\textit{participant} vs.\\ \\textit{participant of} reverses directionality; \\textit{contains} vs.\\ \\textit{located in} inverts containment; \\textit{part of} vs.\\ \\textit{member of} conflates meronymy with membership. a.t.e.\\ = admin.\\ territorial entity.}\n\\label{tab:error_examples}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\section{Schema Label Renaming Experiment}\n\\label{app:renaming}\n\nTo test whether reducing pairwise SCS through label renaming improves accuracy, we conducted a controlled intervention on FewRel 80-relation RE (count 8, Qwen3-32B). We identified 14 high-SCS relation pairs (covering 12 unique relation types, as two types each appear in multiple pairs; mean SCS = 0.87) and replaced one label in each pair with a semantically distinct paraphrase chosen to minimize embedding cosine similarity (all-MiniLM-L6-v2). Examples: \\textit{participant of} $\\to$ \\textit{event involvement} (SCS: 0.97 $\\to$ 0.72), \\textit{follows} $\\to$ \\textit{prior entry} (0.90 $\\to$ 0.69), \\textit{part of} $\\to$ \\textit{integral segment of} (0.90 $\\to$ 0.64). The renaming reduced mean pairwise SCS from 0.87 to 0.67 ($\\Delta = -0.20$). We ran the same 50 BCS-controlled batches (25 low-BCS, 25 high-BCS; 96 samples each) under both original (control) and renamed (treatment) label sets (Table~\\ref{tab:renaming}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h!]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\textbf{Control} & \\textbf{Treatment} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$\\,(pp)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nLow-BCS ($n=25$)  & 90.1 & 87.1 & $-$3.0 \\\\\nHigh-BCS ($n=25$) & 69.3 & 60.5 & $-$8.8 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Accuracy (\\%) under original vs.\\ renamed labels on FewRel count-8 batches. Renaming reduced embedding similarity but decreased accuracy in both groups, with a larger drop on high-BCS batches (Cohen's $d = -2.92$) than low-BCS ($d = -1.09$). Interaction: $\\Delta_{\\text{high}} - \\Delta_{\\text{low}} = -5.9$\\,pp ($t = -7.23$, $p < 0.001$).}\n\\label{tab:renaming}\n\\end{table}\n\nRenaming hurt accuracy uniformly: both groups showed significant declines (paired $t$-test, both $p < 0.001$). The number of renamed types per batch correlated negatively with accuracy change ($r = -0.625$, $p < 0.001$). A solo sanity check confirmed that 11 of 12 renamed labels were individually recognizable (accuracy $\\geq$ original $-5$\\,pp). The results indicate that replacing familiar Wikidata labels with unfamiliar paraphrases disrupts the model's type semantics; this label unfamiliarity effect outweighs any benefit from reduced confusability.\n\n\\section{Relaxed Matching Evaluation (NER)}\n\\label{app:relaxed-matching}\n\nTo test whether exact-match F1 masks the BCS signal in NER by conflating boundary errors with type errors, we evaluate three progressively relaxed matching criteria on the full Few-NERD NER dataset ($n = 450$ batches: 6 count levels $\\times$ 25 batches $\\times$ 3 seeds; Table~\\ref{tab:relaxed_matching}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h!]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Match mode} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$p$} & \\textbf{$\\rho$} & \\textbf{$p$} & \\textbf{F1} \\\\\n\\midrule\nExact        & 0.014 & 0.760 & $-$0.101 & 0.033 & 0.304 \\\\\nRelaxed span & 0.003 & 0.946 & $-$0.110 & 0.020 & 0.346 \\\\\nToken overlap & 0.004 & 0.936 & $-$0.113 & 0.017 & 0.349 \\\\\nType-ignored & 0.023 & 0.621 & $-$0.097 & 0.039 & 0.425 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{BCS--F1 correlation under progressively relaxed NER matching. Relaxed span credits a match if the predicted span overlaps the gold span and the type is correct; token overlap uses token-level intersection; type-ignored drops the type constraint entirely. Relaxed matching improves mean F1 by 4.2\\,pp (98.4\\% of batches improve), but Pearson $r$ remains near zero across all modes (bootstrap 95\\% CI crosses zero in all cases). Boundary errors affect all batches uniformly and do not mask a BCS signal.}\n\\label{tab:relaxed_matching}\n\\end{table}\n\nA per-error-mode analysis confirms this null extends to all individual categories: miss ($\\rho = 0.017$), boundary ($\\rho = -0.043$), and type substitution ($\\rho = -0.010$) rates show no BCS association (all $p > 0.3$, $n = 450$). The null holds within each count level (count 2: $r = 0.022$; count 6: $r = 0.110$; count 12: $r = 0.028$; all $p > 0.3$), ruling out count as a confound.\n\n\\end{document}\n",
      "numLines": 406,
      "startLine": 500,
      "totalLines": 905
    }
  }
}

7	
8	\section{Prompt Templates}
9	\label{sec:appendix_prompts}
10	
11	This appendix reproduces the complete prompt templates used for all three extraction tasks.
12	All prompts are injected into a single \texttt{user} message (no system prompt); the model's chat template adds the system/assistant framing.
13	Both versions provide a JSON-formatted type schema to the vLLM constrained-decoding engine; the schema is \emph{not} included in the natural-language prompt itself.
14	We use greedy decoding (\texttt{temperature=0}, \texttt{seed=42}) throughout.
15	
16	\medskip\noindent
17	\textbf{Notation.}
18	Placeholders are shown in \texttt{\{braces\}}.
19	Text \textbf{added in v2} (relative to v1) is marked with $\triangleright$.
20	Text \textbf{removed in v2} is marked with \sout{strikethrough}.
21	
22	\subsection{Named Entity Recognition (Few-NERD)}
23	
24	\paragraph{v1 prompt (with character offsets).}
25	
26	\begin{small}
27	\begin{lstlisting}
28	Extract all named entities from the following text.
29	Only extract entities of the following types:
30	- {type_1}: {definition_1}
31	- {type_2}: {definition_2}
32	  ...
33	
34	Text: {input_text}
35	
36	Output as JSON: [{"text": "...", "type": "...",
37	                  "start": ..., "end": ...}]
38	\end{lstlisting}
39	\end{small}
40	
41	\noindent
42	\textbf{v1 constrained-decoding schema:}
43	\begin{small}
44	\begin{lstlisting}
45	{"type": "array",
46	 "items": {"type": "object",
47	   "properties": {
48	     "text":  {"type": "string"},
49	     "type":  {"type": "string",
50	               "enum": [&lt;sorted_types>]},
51	     "start": {"type": "integer"},
52	     "end":   {"type": "integer"}},
53	   "required": ["text","type","start","end"]}}
54	\end{lstlisting}
55	\end{small}
56	
57	\paragraph{v2 prompt (text+type only).}
58	
59	\begin{small}
60	\begin{lstlisting}
61	Extract all named entities from the following text.
62	Only extract entities of the following types:
63	- {type_1}: {definition_1}
64	- {type_2}: {definition_2}
65	  ...
66	
67	Text: {input_text}
68	\end{lstlisting}
69	\end{small}
70	\vspace{-0.5em}
71	\noindent$\triangleright$~\textbf{Added in v2:}
72	\begin{small}
73	\begin{lstlisting}
74	Only extract entities that clearly match one of the
75	given types. If no entities of these types exist in
76	the text, output an empty list [].
77	\end{lstlisting}
78	\end{small}
79	\vspace{-0.5em}
80	\begin{small}
81	\begin{lstlisting}
82	Output as JSON: [{"text": "...", "type": "..."}]
83	\end{lstlisting}
84	\end{small}
85	
86	\noindent
87	\textbf{v2 constrained-decoding schema:}
88	\begin{small}
89	\begin{lstlisting}
90	{"type": "array",
91	 "items": {"type": "object",
92	   "properties": {
93	     "text": {"type": "string"},
94	     "type": {"type": "string",
95	              "enum": [&lt;sorted_types>]}},
96	   "required": ["text","type"]}}
97	\end{lstlisting}
98	\end{small}
99	
100	\paragraph{v1 $\to$ v2 differences (NER).}
101	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
102	\item \textbf{Offset fields removed.} The \texttt{start}/\texttt{end} integer fields are dropped from both the natural-language instruction and the JSON schema. In v1, 96.5\% of predicted offsets were hallucinated (\S\ref{sec:model}).
103	\item \textbf{Format constraints added.} Two sentences are appended: (a)~``Only extract entities that clearly match one of the given types'' suppresses spurious predictions; (b)~``If no entities \ldots output an empty list~\texttt{[]}'' provides an explicit empty-output path.
104	\end{enumerate}
105	
106	\subsection{Event Detection (MAVEN)}
107	
108	\paragraph{v1 prompt (with character offsets).}
109	
110	\begin{small}
111	\begin{lstlisting}
112	Extract all event triggers from the following text.
113	A trigger is the word or phrase that most clearly
114	expresses the event occurrence. Only extract triggers
115	of the following event types:
116	- {type_1}: {definition_1}
117	- {type_2}: {definition_2}
118	  ...
119	
120	Text: {input_text}
121	
122	Output as JSON: [{"text": "...", "type": "...",
123	                  "start": ..., "end": ...}]
124	\end{lstlisting}
125	\end{small}
126	
127	\paragraph{v2 prompt (text+type only).}
128	
129	\begin{small}
130	\begin{lstlisting}
131	Extract all event triggers from the following text.
132	A trigger is the word or phrase that most clearly
133	expresses the event occurrence. Only extract triggers
134	of the following event types:
135	- {type_1}: {definition_1}
136	- {type_2}: {definition_2}
137	  ...
138	
139	Text: {input_text}
140	\end{lstlisting}
141	\end{small}
142	\vspace{-0.5em}
143	\noindent$\triangleright$~\textbf{Added in v2:}
144	\begin{small}
145	\begin{lstlisting}
146	Only extract triggers that clearly match one of the
147	given event types. If no events of these types exist
148	in the text, output an empty list [].
149	\end{lstlisting}
150	\end{small}
151	\vspace{-0.5em}
152	\begin{small}
153	\begin{lstlisting}
154	Output as JSON: [{"text": "...", "type": "..."}]
155	\end{lstlisting}
156	\end{small}
157	
158	\noindent The JSON schemas for ED are identical to the NER schemas above; only the natural-language framing differs (``event triggers'' instead of ``named entities'').
159	
160	\paragraph{v1 $\to$ v2 differences (ED).}
161	Same two changes as NER: offset fields removed, format constraints added.
162	
163	\subsection{Relation Extraction (FewRel)}
164	
165	RE uses only a v2-style prompt (no offset fields are applicable to classification).
166	
167	\paragraph{Prompt.}
168	
169	\begin{small}
170	\begin{lstlisting}
171	Given the following sentence and two entities,
172	classify the relation between them.
173	
174	Sentence: "{sentence}"
175	Head entity: "{head_entity}"
176	Tail entity: "{tail_entity}"
177	
178	Candidate relations: {type_1}, {type_2}, ...
179	
180	Output a single JSON object:
181	  {"relation": "&lt;relation_type>"}
182	If none of the candidate relations apply, output:
183	  {"relation": "none"}
184	\end{lstlisting}
185	\end{small}
186	
187	\noindent
188	\textbf{Constrained-decoding schema:}
189	\begin{small}
190	\begin{lstlisting}
191	{"type": "object",
192	 "properties": {
193	   "relation": {"type": "string",
194	     "enum": [&lt;sorted_types>, "none"]}},
195	 "required": ["relation"]}
196	\end{lstlisting}
197	\end{small}
198	
199	\subsection{Type Definitions}
200	\label{sec:appendix_type_defs}
201	
202	All prompts include per-type natural-language definitions sourced from the respective dataset documentation:
203	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
204	\item \textbf{Few-NERD:} 66 fine-grained entity types with definitions derived from the Few-NERD type ontology \citep{fewnerd-2021}.
205	\item \textbf{MAVEN:} 168 event types with FrameNet-based definitions \citep{maven-2020}.
206	\item \textbf{FewRel:} 80 relation types with Wikidata property descriptions \citep{fewrel-2018}.
207	\end{itemize}
208	
209	\noindent Type definitions are injected as ``\texttt{- \{type\_name\}: \{definition\}}'' lines in alphabetical order. The number of types per prompt varies by experimental condition (count${}= 1$ to full schema; \S\ref{sec:exp_design}).
210

154	
155	\section{SDGD Full Protocol and Results}
156	\label{sec:appendix_sdgd}
157	
158	We construct a thresholded confusability graph $G = (V, E)$ where each type is a vertex and an edge connects types $t_i, t_j$ whenever $\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) > \tau$. Greedy graph coloring (\textsc{largest\_first}) assigns each type to a color such that no two adjacent (i.e., highly confusable) types share a color. Each color class forms one extraction batch, and predictions are merged via set union.
159	
160	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
161	\item Compute pairwise SCS matrix $S_{ij} = \cos(\text{embed}(t_i), \text{embed}(t_j))$.
162	\item Construct graph $G$: add edge $(t_i, t_j)$ iff $S_{ij} > \tau$.
163	\item Apply greedy graph coloring (\textsc{largest\_first}) to assign each type a color $c(t)$.
164	\item For each color $k$, form batch $B_k = \{t : c(t) = k\}$ and run extraction on all test instances.
165	\item Merge: union of per-batch predictions (v1) or confidence-gated selection (v2).
166	\end{enumerate}
167	Oracle routing (Table~\ref{tab:routing_strategies}) recovers substantial accuracy (72.4\% vs.\ 50.4\% single-pass), confirming the sub-schemas contain correct predictions; the failure lies in routing, not decomposition quality.
168	
169	We compare four modes: (i)~\textbf{single\_pass}, the undecomposed baseline; (ii)~\textbf{sdgd\_zero}, SCS-guided partitioning; (iii)~\textbf{random\_batch}, random partitioning with matched batch sizes; and (iv)~\textbf{per\_type}, the extreme case where each type is extracted in isolation.
170	
171	Single-pass extraction achieves F1\,=\,0.445 (P\,=\,0.563, R\,=\,0.368). SDGD obtains F1\,=\,0.251 (P\,=\,0.210, R\,=\,0.314), a 44\% relative drop. Random batching reaches F1\,=\,0.320 (P\,=\,0.249, R\,=\,0.446), and per-type extraction yields F1\,=\,0.292 (P\,=\,0.194, R\,=\,0.586). Every decomposition mode falls below the single-pass baseline (Table~\ref{tab:sdgd_results}).
172	
173	\begin{table}[h]
174	\centering
175	\small
176	\caption{Few-NERD extraction under decomposition modes. V1 uses union merge; v2 uses confidence-gated merge with \texttt{min\_batch\_size=8} (reducing 8$\to$4 batches for sdgd\_zero). All decomposition modes produce lower F1 than the single-pass baseline. All modes use the v1 prompt.}
177	\label{tab:sdgd_results}
```

207:\caption{Routing strategy comparison on FewRel 80-relation RE with graph-coloring sub-schema decomposition. Oracle requires gold labels. All practical (non-oracle) strategies perform below the single-pass baseline. The failure stems from constrained decoding (enum constraint), which forces confident predictions in every sub-schema: 73.4\% of instances receive positive predictions from 3--5 sub-schemas simultaneously, with per-sub-schema none-rates of only 20--40\%.}
239:\caption{TOST sensitivity analysis across equivalence margins. The interaction effect (0.47\,pp, 90\% CI [$-$1.0, 2.0]\,pp) is bounded below 2\,pp at marginal significance ($p = 0.047$); the $\pm 3$\,pp choice does not drive the equivalence conclusion. At $\pm 1$\,pp, equivalence is inconclusive ($p = 0.278$), meaning interaction could reach approximately half the main effect magnitude.}
285:\caption{MAVEN per-type BCS--F1 correlation before and after controlling for centrality confounders. Raw correlations are Spearman $\rho$; partial correlations are Pearson $r$ from residualized regression. Degree centrality is computed on the SCS graph at threshold 0.7. All values from experiment \texttt{exp\_maven\_residualized\_bcs}.}
316:\caption{BCS--accuracy partial correlation controlling for degree centrality. FewRel shows minimal attenuation (VIF${}\,=\,1.18$, low collinearity). DocRED shows high BCS--centrality collinearity (VIF${}\,=\,50.9$, $r = 0.99$), but the partial correlation remains highly significant.}
341:\caption{Batch composition on FewRel 80-relation RE with oracle routing. $^\dagger$Mean $\pm$ SD across 15 random seeds.}
381:\caption{BCS--accuracy Pearson correlation across four prompt variants. At count 32, all four variants show negative BCS--accuracy correlations ($r \leq -0.47$), with two reaching $p < 0.05$. At count 8, the narrower BCS range reduces statistical power.}
409:\caption{BCS--accuracy regression under mean vs.\ max pairwise SCS aggregation (FewRel, 16-relation batches). Permutation test for slope equivalence across pools: MEAN $p \approx 0.60$; MAX $p \approx 0.44$. The 80-pool row is identical under both definitions because these batches were collected using mean-aggregated BCS; the MAX column reflects the same data for completeness.}
433:\caption{BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. Low-BCS batches contain maximally dissimilar types; high-BCS batches contain maximally similar types. The 80-relation extreme-batch gap reflects the wider BCS range; regression slopes are consistent across pools ($p = 0.60$; Table~\ref{tab:within_pool}; see \S\ref{sec:practical_magnitude} for typical-batch magnitudes). The 16-relation condition used a single extreme batch per BCS level; bootstrap CIs require multiple batches.}
458:\caption{Paired comparison of constrained vs.\ unconstrained decoding on 25 identical FewRel batch type sets. Both formats yield virtually identical BCS--accuracy correlations (Fisher $z \approx 0$), confirming the confusability effect is format-independent. Mean accuracy is also unchanged ($\Delta = -0.08$\,pp), ruling out a format-driven accuracy shift.}
483:\caption{Per-batch accuracy (\%) under constrained and unconstrained decoding on 25 identical FewRel batch type sets (count 8, 96 instances each). 23/25 batches yield identical accuracy; the two exceptions differ by exactly one sample ($\Delta = -1.04$\,pp). L = low-BCS, H = high-BCS.}
528:\caption{Three-way BCS--accuracy analysis on FewRel. Slope: OLS of batch accuracy on BCS (bootstrap CIs, $B{=}10{,}000$). $\Delta$: accuracy gap between top- and bottom-quartile BCS batches. See Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_within_pool} for CI interpretation.}
559:\caption{Pearson correlation between SCS and empirical confusion rate. ``Def'' concatenates type name with definition before embedding. All correlations significant ($p < 0.001$). The inconsistent direction of $\Delta r$ indicates that definitions provide no systematic benefit.}
609:\caption{Pairwise confusion prediction: SCS vs.\ simple baselines. Spearman $\rho$ between each metric and the empirical confusion rate. SCS outperforms all baselines on FewRel; the margin is smaller on Few-NERD, where hierarchical naming conventions provide partial signal to string-based metrics. $^\dagger$42\% of pairs lack WordNet coverage.}
649:\caption{Variance decomposition on FewRel (3 seeds $\times$ 25 batches per count level). Batch SD: root-mean intra-seed variance averaged over seeds. Seed SD: standard deviation of per-seed means. Ratio = batch variance / seed variance. At count 80, only one batch composition exists, so batch variance vanishes by construction.}
681:\caption{Cross-model count curves for LLaMA-3.1-70B-Instruct (FP8) and Qwen3-32B. RE decline (count 8$\to$80): $-$30.7\,pp (70B) vs.\ $-$30.1\,pp (32B). NER decline (count 2$\to$22): $-$7.1\,pp (70B) vs.\ $-$23.6\,pp (32B); both models exhibit a U-shaped rebound at higher counts. 25 batches per count level, same samples and batch compositions across models (seed 42). All metrics are micro-averaged across batches.}
721:\caption{Cross-model count curves for Gemini 2.5 Flash (API) and Qwen3-32B on FewRel RE (classification accuracy). 25 batches per count level, same samples and batch compositions (seed 42). Gemini's count-1 result is included for completeness; Qwen3 count-1 was not collected. Both models show monotonic decline from count 4 onward ($\rho = -1.0$ for both on counts 4--32). $^\dagger$Count 64: Gemini based on 11/25 batches (partial).}
749:\caption{Cross-model BCS-controlled FewRel RE summary. All models show significant low-vs-high BCS accuracy gaps. Gap magnitude varies with model capacity (ceiling compression), but the direction is consistent across open-weight (8B--70B) and proprietary architectures.}
772:\caption{BCS gap under few-shot prompting (FewRel, count 8). Demonstrations improve absolute accuracy on low-BCS batches (+3.3--7.2\,pp over 0-shot) but provide inconsistent benefit on high-BCS batches (+5.6\,pp at 1-shot, $-$1.0\,pp at 5-shot). The confusability gap widens from +18.5\,pp (0-shot) to +22.8\,pp (5-shot). All metrics are micro-averaged across batches.}

820:[Omitted long matching line]
843:\caption{Illustrative confusion instances from FewRel 80-rel RE. All gold-predicted pairs have SCS\,$>$\,0.90: \textit{participant} vs.\ \textit{participant of} reverses directionality; \textit{contains} vs.\ \textit{located in} inverts containment; \textit{part of} vs.\ \textit{member of} conflates meronymy with membership. a.t.e.\ = admin.\ territorial entity.}
866:\caption{Accuracy (\%) under original vs.\ renamed labels on FewRel count-8 batches. Renaming reduced embedding similarity but decreased accuracy in both groups, with a larger drop on high-BCS batches (Cohen's $d = -2.92$) than low-BCS ($d = -1.09$). Interaction: $\Delta_{\text{high}} - \Delta_{\text{low}} = -5.9$\,pp ($t = -7.23$, $p < 0.001$).}
